- **One block in the specification was written the old way** — `calculate_physics(obj: Object)`, which does `obj.x += obj.velocity` — and says `mut` now. `examples/` had none: every mutating call in it is on `self` or on a local, which is also why nothing there could have found the `mutates` hole below.
- **Eleven tests in `crates/nikaia/tests/mut_parameters.rs`**, two of them compiled and run. Half are about the refusal *not* firing: a read, a shadowed name, a method nothing describes — because the other half of C.1 is C.4, and a correct program refused is the worse of the two mistakes.

### Fixed (a method that changes its subject is a column now)

- **The ledger had no way to say `&mut self`.** Its type language spells a view `&T` and has no second spelling for a mutable one, so `Vec::push` and `Vec::len` wrote the same receiver type. Harmless while nothing read it; the moment [ADR-094](docs/specification/adr/adr-094.md) D1 began writing a `&` off the `keeps` column, `fn fill(out: Vec[i64]) { out.push(1) }` lent `out` and `rustc` answered *cannot borrow `*out` as mutable* about a file nobody wrote.
- **`mutates` is the claim**, seven of `std`'s ninety-four entries — `Vec::push`, `Vec::sort`, `Vec::sort_by_key`, `String::push`, `String::push_str`, `HashMap::entry`, `Args::nth`. For a function this compiler reads the body of it is not inferred at all: `&mut self` is the declaration, which is D3's sentence one position over. `Locked::set` and the other hull doors are `&self` in the language below and are untouched.
- **No example could have found it**: every mutating call in `examples/` is on `self` or on a local, never on a parameter. The test is in `crates/nikaia/tests/lent_arguments.rs`, beside its reading twin.

### Fixed (three holes this step walked into, each older than it)

- **`contracts::sync` never walked into a `throw`.** `throw ConfigError::NotFound(path)` was a statement the walk stepped over, so the `path` inside it was neither read nor kept — and the moment callers stopped writing their own `&`, the argument was handed over by value. Walking it then exposed the second one.
- **A variant constructor was read as a callee nothing describes.** `E::V(x)` for a declared enum or struct is not a function and has no ledger entry, and the fail-closed answer for an unknown callee — *it keeps what it is given* — is the wrong one there. `reached` now says nothing about it.
- **The emitter's key for a qualified callee was the aliased path.** `use http as h` made `h::id_of(made)` look up `h::id_of` while the checker had recorded `http::id_of`, so the answer was never found. It is unaliased at both ends now — a hole `nullable_args` had shared since it was written, which is why no test had caught it.

### Added (thirteen tests for the call half)

- **`crates/nikaia/tests/lent_arguments.rs`**, five of them compiled and **run**, because the declaration and the call are written by two different passes off one column and the only proof they agree is the language below accepting both at once. Most of the file is about the claim being **withheld** — the kept parameter, the copy type, the argument already a view, the method call, the callee no signature describes — since a version of this rule that lent everywhere would pass the first test and break the language. One of them is the desynchronisation itself: an argument whose type the checker could not pin is still lent, because the declaration was.

### Changed (a grammar is entered by a call, and the silent entry choice is gone)

- **[ADR-082](docs/specification/adr/adr-082.md) D1 and D2, built in one change.** `Json.value(input)` enters a grammar, every `pub` rule is an entry, and `dsl X from e` is refused at the parse with a message naming the call — the shape [ADR-022](docs/specification/adr/adr-022.md) gave `fn:`. One form in the language at every moment; no deprecation window with two spellings for one thing.
- **The defect went with it, and that is what this was for.** `entry_rule` picked a grammar's entry itself — *the first `pub` rule, and a `par_fold` one ahead of an earlier one* — so a grammar with two of them got one by source order, in silence. It is **deleted** rather than narrowed, and `a_grammar_with_two_public_rules_is_entered_by_either` is a grammar where the old choice would have taken the wrong one whichever was asked for.
- **Nothing in the parser had to learn a shape.** `Json.value(input)` was already a method call in this language's grammar; what stopped it was `NK1117` — *nothing declares `Json`* — so what it needed was a checker that counts a grammar name as declared and an emitter that recognises the receiver. A grammar name can only stand where a callee stands, so there is nothing to disambiguate it against.
- **And the `catch` beside an entry is answered from a contract now.** Every `pub` rule is an entry in the ledger with `throws = ["?"]`, because a rule past a commit point can fail ([ADR-023](docs/specification/adr/adr-023.md) D9) — so [ADR-091](docs/specification/adr/adr-091.md) D4's one line telling `NK1134` that a `dsl … from …` can fail is **deleted with the form it was for**, which is what that record said would happen to it.
- **Two things the record had not said.** The **preamble** is decided by the entry and was still deciding by the old rule: `uses_driver` asked whether the *picked* rule is a `par_fold`, and with the rule named at the call it has to ask about the rule named — the lowering was right, the preamble was not, and `rustc` answered *"use of undeclared type `ParseContext`"* about the generated file. And a rule that is **not `pub`** needs its own sentence: `Two.N(data)` is an entry naming a private rule rather than a method call that missed, and *there is no such rule*, about one written three lines up, is a message a reader cannot act on.
- **Migrated in the same change**: nine lines in eight programs (`1brc`, `access-log`, `calc`, `config`, `inventory/stock`, `json`, `k-nucleotide`, `report`), `examples/README.md`, Part II's own blocks and its `@frame` example, and the three test files. `grep -rn "dsl [A-Za-z_]* from"` over `examples/`, `tests/` and `crates/` finds only the refusal's own message.
- **Four tests added to `crates/nikaia/tests/grammar_lowering.rs`**, and the citations in `open-work.md` moved with the entry that closed: five sentences named an entry by its **number**, which is what that page's own head says not to do, and each now names its subject.

### Decided (a package is found by version through Cargo, under `nikaia_<name>`)

- **[ADR-103](docs/specification/adr/adr-103.md).** `http = "1.2"` in `nikaia.toml` resolves on crates.io as the crate `nikaia_http`, through Cargo's own dependency rename, so the prefix appears in one generated line and never in a `.nika` file or a `use`. `"1.2"` is Cargo's semver, the resolved version is the lockfile column ADR-021 already has, a `git` table with a `tag` is the same arm without an index, and the path arm stays. A published crate holds the manifest, the sources and the ledger, plus a stub `Cargo.toml` that exists only for the resolver; the consumer reads and emits it as it does a path dependency. A crate under the prefix without sources and ledger is refused by name.
- **What it answers:** `open-decisions.md`'s first entry, how a package is named by a version, which leaves the file — the registry, the version grammar and the lockfile were already in the tool every build runs, and the one thing to decide was the name.
- **Nothing of it is built**; `open-work.md` carries the four steps.

### Fixed (removing `dsl … from …` is a work order, not a question)

- [ADR-082](docs/specification/adr/adr-082.md) §5 now says plainly what is built (nothing), what the live defect is (a grammar with two `pub` rules gets one of them by source order), and the one change that closes it: the call form built, a `throws` on every generated entry, the old form removed and refused with the new one in the message, and everything that writes it migrated in the same commit — eight programs, `examples/README.md`, Part II 10.7's own block, three test files. The record had carried a corrected count and a question about whether to deprecate first; the question leaves `open-decisions.md`, because the record had already answered it and only the size of the migration had been miscounted. `open-work.md`'s entry is the work order, with the steps and what *done* means.
### Changed (a `for` lends, and a `let` over a place is a view of it)

- **[ADR-094](docs/specification/adr/adr-094.md) D4, step 2 of five — and the first step that changes what programs mean.** `for e in entries { … }` used to take `entries` away; it leaves it where it was now, so `entries.len()` on the next line is a program rather than `rustc`'s *use of moved value* about a file nobody wrote. `let s = totals.stations[name]` and `let name = config.name` are views, because the language below refuses to move a value out of a container or out of a borrowed field — a move there was never what the line meant.
- **Off the shape of the expression and not off a column**, which is why the step needed no ledger: a **place** is lent, and a call, a range or a literal owns what it made. `for i in 0..3` and `for line in io::lines()` are untouched.
- **Three things the record had not said, each found on the corpus rather than reasoned about.** **`.iter()` and not `&`**: the iterated name may already *be* a view — `examples/inventory/stock.nika`'s `total(entries: &Vec[Entry])` — and `&entries` is then `&&Vec<Entry>`, which Rust does not iterate, while `.iter()` reads the same through any number of references and the emitter has no types to tell them apart ([ADR-028](docs/specification/adr/adr-028.md)). **`drain()` had to be built**: D4 names `for x in xs.drain()` as the written form of taking the elements away and `std` had no such entry, so a body that hands an element to a callee which *keeps* it had no way to say so — `or_insert(counts)` in `access-log.nika`, `all.push(v)` in `json.nika`, four loops in all. It is `into_iter` below, because D4's words are *removing a name from scope*. **And a `let` over a place that copies must not lend**: `let mi = self.bodies[i].mass` over an `f64` is a copy, and a `&` there is a borrow held across the loop that writes the same field — `E0502`, met in `examples/n-body.nika`.
- **So the `let` half asks the checker and the `for` half does not.** Which of the two a place is, is a question about the **type**, and the emitter has none — so `Checked::lent_lets` answers it with the same `moves_away` that `NK2101` reads, and where it cannot type the place it says nothing. That silence is where every program already is, and it is the reason the new refusal is narrower than the record's sentence: **`NK1137`** refuses a `&` written in front of a `for`'s list, and not yet one in a `let`, because there the written `&` is still a program's only way to say what the line means. `open-work.md` carries that limit as the next thing.
- **Eight tests in `crates/nikaia/tests/lending.rs`**, three of them compiled and **run** — the loop that leaves its list behind, a `for` over a parameter that is already a view, and a `drain()` that hands its elements to a `push`.
- **The corpus moved eight lines**: four loops gained `.drain()`, two `&` came off (`report.nika`'s loop head and `n-body.nika`'s `let`), and two `&` stayed, at the two places the checker cannot type yet.
- **And the diagnostics catalogue was four codes behind before this one.** `NK1134`, `NK1135` and `NK1136` were raised by the compiler and catalogued nowhere; Part III's table carries all four now, and its **Status** note says `NK1101`–`NK1137` rather than a range that stopped at `NK1133`. A code the compiler reports and the specification does not name is the same defect as a stale Status note ([`docs/README.md`](docs/README.md) §1), one position over.

### Decided (a parameter may be a function, and the type says what it may do)

- **[ADR-102](docs/specification/adr/adr-102.md).** `fn(Request) -> Response` is a type, spelled as a signature is, with `sync` and `throws` after the result. Without `sync` the code may pause and without `throws` it cannot fail — the reading a declaration has. A lambda that does less fits a type that allows more; a pausing lambda handed to `fn() sync` is refused here, where today `rustc` refuses it about a file nobody wrote. Whether the callee runs the parameter or keeps it is inferred — the same `keeps` question [ADR-094](docs/specification/adr/adr-094.md) asks of every parameter — and only a kept handler's promises come from the type; a run lambda's still decide the callee's own, as `map`'s do. `@detached` is never written.
- **What it answers:** how a package receives a handler. `examples/http/` can declare `route`, and `open-decisions.md`'s entry narrows to the one thing left — a bound naming a trait in another package.
- **Nothing of it is built**; `open-work.md` carries the four steps.

### Added (the `keeps` column, and what it says about the corpus)

- **[ADR-094](docs/specification/adr/adr-094.md) step 1 of five.** Whether a parameter is lent or handed over is the callee's, and this is the analysis that answers it: `contracts::keeps` infers, per function, which parameters the body **keeps** — stores into a struct, assigns into a place, hands back by value, gives to a task, or passes to a callee whose own parameter keeps it — and the ledger records it beside `returns`. **Nothing reads the column yet**, which is what that step is for: the answer could be diffed against the corpus before one call site changed.
- **What the corpus says, and it is the measurement the step exists for.** Of **39 functions across `examples/`, six keep a parameter**; the other 33 read what they are given. Among the 33 is `report.nika`'s `page(entries, total)` — the function whose own file carries a comment explaining that `count` has to be read first because `page` *"consumes"* the entries. It does not, and the column says so. That comment is one of the two pieces of evidence ADR-094 was written on.
- **And `std` says which of its own keep: six entries of ninety-four** — `Vec::push`, `String::push`, `HashMap::entry`, `Entry::or_insert`, and the two `set` doors, written by hand and reviewed like code the way that file's Rust half already is. **An absent `keeps` on a *present* entry means it keeps nothing**, which is `std.contracts`' own convention for `sync` said once more for a second column. A file that recorded nothing and left every caller to assume the worst would have been safe and would have left the language exactly where it is — the ratio *is* the feature.
- **A least fixpoint, where `sync`'s is a greatest one**, and the difference is which direction is safe: `sync` is a promise and is taken away on doubt, `keeps` is a restriction and is added on doubt. So two functions that pass each other a parameter neither stores keep neither — which a greatest fixpoint would have got wrong here exactly as a least one would have got mutual recursion wrong there.
- **Fail closed means *keeps*.** A callee no ledger describes, a method name no entry carries, a `spawn` inside a `spawn` — each counts as keeping, because the two wrong answers are not symmetric: saying *kept* of a value that is only read costs a caller an owned argument, which is where every caller already is, and saying *lent* of a value the body stores emits a `&T` parameter whose body moves it — `rustc`'s error about a file nobody wrote ([Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)).
- **Which entry a method call goes to is still the type checker's answer and not this file's** ([ADR-028](docs/specification/adr/adr-028.md)). A weaker question is asked instead, the way `touches` already asks it: if **no** entry named `::push` keeps its argument, this call keeps none whatever the receiver turns out to be. That is what lets `rows.push(row)` keep and `rows.len()` lend without a single type.
- **Eleven tests in `crates/nikaia/tests/keeps.rs`, and half of them are about the claim being withheld** — a read stays a read, a view returned is not kept, a callee that only reads leaves its caller lending, mutual recursion keeps nothing. An analysis that answered *keeps* everywhere would be safe and useless, and nothing in the column itself says which of the two it is.
- **One thing step 1 leaves for step 3**, written down rather than left to be met: a *copy* type reported as kept is the rule applied and not a miss. `fn add(&mut self, temp: i32) { self.min = temp }` keeps `temp`, the value does outlive the call, and what that costs is nothing — deciding that it costs nothing is the emitter's question.

### Decided (an error that newly reaches a `catch` is named once)

- **[ADR-101](docs/specification/adr/adr-101.md).** Every failure is caught or declared, and that is checked; what a `catch { … }` did in silence was take a callee's *new* failure. When a `throws` set grows, every handler over that callee is named in the build output, `--locked` fails until the ledger is regenerated and committed, and the commit is the acknowledgement — nothing is written at the handler, and a handler that matches on `error` is told the same as one that does not. The syntax of `catch` is unchanged. `open-decisions.md`'s entry on whether `catch` takes a pattern leaves the file: the guarantee did not need one.
- **Nothing of it is built**, and nothing can be until error types are lowered as enums — `std.contracts` writes `throws = ["?"]` on every entry. `open-work.md` carries it beside the `NK2401` machinery it reuses.
### Changed (the untrusted path goes back to being a question)

- **[ADR-058](docs/specification/adr/adr-058.md) D7 is [`open-decisions.md`](docs/open-decisions.md)'s sixth entry**, and the feature itself is in doubt rather than merely unscheduled. `open-work.md` had been carrying it as *"the one piece that does not wait"* and *"the only entry that closes a security hole rather than an ergonomic one"*. The second half stands; the reasons given for picking it up first do not.
- **Two false sentences, both measured the moment somebody picked the work up.** It said D7 *"costs no new analysis"* — `contracts::trust` is a **whole-program join**, one answer for the program, and D7 needs a **per-value** one, a dataflow analysis over locals that happens to read the same lattice. And it said the rule was *"testable against `fs::map` today"* — `std.contracts` carries **no** `provenance = "untrusted"` entry at all, and the one untrusted source that needs no server (`fs::map(path; trusted: false)`, [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D3) is not built: the ledger parses the word and no call site may write it.
- **And the urgency argument does not hold for this one.** `open-work.md` §2's rule — *a refusal is free before programs exist and breaking afterwards* — is why every other unbuilt refusal on that page is urgent. A refusal about untrusted values cannot reject a program until some value **is** untrusted, and what makes one untrusted is the request, which is the server. The day it could break a program is the day it becomes possible to write one, so no free window is being lost.
- **Four ways out, with what each costs**, including withdrawing D7 outright — which would take [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D8's claim that the trust lattice **generalises** with it, since D7 is the second consumer that argument was staked on. The fourth is `Untrusted[T]` as a type rather than a pass: the same guarantee paid for in the type system, which needs no analysis and cannot silently stop working, at the price of every signature that handles a name.
- **The runtime cost of every option but one is zero**, which is the question that sent this back: D7 is a compile-time refusal and not a check. What costs anything at runtime is `fs::within` itself — one path resolution and a prefix comparison per name, which is the check a server performs anyway.

### Fixed (a consumer reads a dependency's ledger, and the false `NK1129` is gone)

- **[ADR-100](docs/specification/adr/adr-100.md) D1, D3, D4 and D5 — everything but D6.** `open-work.md`'s oldest defect is closed in both of its shapes: a trait method whose only call is in the file next door, and **a handler whose body calls the package it implements against**. `impl lib::Greeter for Fixed { fn greet(&self) -> String { return lib::hello() } }` compiles and runs.
- **The ordering is the fix, not a bigger inference.** A package's ledger is now written in *that package's own root* by that package's own build, and the build is ordered dependencies-first (`project::dependencies_first`, Kahn's over the members — reversing `members_of` is not a topological order once a package is reached two ways). So `http`'s build has `deeper` in view and writes the answer down; `app` reads it. **That is why the reverted settling pass was the wrong shape rather than an incomplete one** — it derived on the consumer's side, where the graph is smaller by decision ([ADR-053](docs/specification/adr/adr-053.md) D3), and two builds of one library then disagreed about whether `ok` pauses. `the_rules_that_come_with_a_path_dependency` is the test that failed under it and passes under this.
- **The ledger header carries a `[sources]` table**: one unit by file name, and the SHA-256 of its bytes — the same digest the cache key already computes ([ADR-021](docs/specification/adr/adr-021.md)). A consumer believes a dependency's ledger while the hashes match and derives that package again where they do not, so **a stale ledger is a hash that does not match, never a belief**. The file name and not a path, because a path would put the machine that built it into a file Part III 13.5 makes a pure function of the source tree.
- **Three ways of not being believed, and each is a test.** A hash that moved, a unit that arrived or left, a ledger with no `[sources]` at all, and a ledger that will not parse. The last two are derivations rather than errors: it is a generated file a person may have edited, and *nothing was recorded* must not read as *nothing changed* — [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D1's polarity, which is the whole reason believing is safe at all.
- **A believed ledger hands over the package's own entries and nothing further.** A library's ledger is its build's record, so it carries its own dependencies' entries under their names; absorbing those under the consumer's word for the library produced `lib::c::Id`, a type nothing declares and no program can write. `Ledger::published` drops them, which is what makes the believed answer the **same** answer the derived one was rather than a larger one. Found by `a_package_reached_through_two_parents_is_one_package` failing, which is a real diamond and not a fixture built for this.
- **`--locked` gained the check D4 describes, and it cost one line**: every member's ledger is compared byte for byte instead of rewritten, so changing a dependency's source and running `nikaia build --locked` names *that dependency's* ledger. Measured on a two-package probe.
- **`Ledger::infer_package` takes the library now**, which the older doc comment said it must not: a ledger inferred against a different library would be a different file for the same source. D1 and D5 answer that rather than ignore it — a dependency's ledger is a **build input**, with one author, existing before its consumer is checked. The reasoning is kept beside the change rather than deleted.
- **Fifteen tests**: eight in `crates/nikaia/tests/dependency_ledger.rs` for the believe-or-derive decision and the round trip, one in `tests/project.rs` that builds and **runs** the two-package handler and then checks the dependency's own ledger is where it should be, and the six already in `package_graph.rs`. `open-work.md` §1 is empty again; what was there is quoted in its place, because a defect that sat for rounds behind a question is worth a sentence about why.

### Fixed (the inference graph is the package, not the file)

- **[ADR-100](docs/specification/adr/adr-100.md) D2, the first of that record's five steps.** `modules::Program::of` called `Ledger::infer` once per **file**, so every fixpoint the ledger rests on stopped at the file boundary — and a callee in the file next door was in neither `own` nor `std`, which `reach_of` sets `blocked` for. `Sync::No` means *"can pause **or** could not be vouched for"* and every reader takes the first, so `NK1129` refused `impl Simple for Thing { fn go(&self) -> i64 { return plain(self.n) } }` for a body whose only call is `n + 1` two files away. It compiles and runs now.
- **Three columns and not one**, which is what said the boundary was the bug rather than the trait rule. Measured on one probe, old binary against new: `sync = "inferred"` where the column had been absent for four functions, `throws = ["LeereZeile"]` where it had been `["?"]`, and `touches = ["stdout write"]` where it had been the empty list. **The last one is the sharpest**, because its polarity runs the other way: an empty `touches` means *touches everything* ([ADR-033](docs/specification/adr/adr-033.md) D4), so an unresolved call did not read as an unknown — it read as an answer nobody could use, and two `overlap` branches over such a function ordered against each other for nothing.
- **`sharing` stayed per unit, deliberately.** It summarises the `Shared` values a body holds rather than folding a call graph, so there is no fixpoint to run across files; what it needed from its neighbours was the callee's `signature`, and that is in the package-wide ledger the declaration loop now builds. The same reasoning the other way decided `borrowing_structs`: a `Symbol` is interned by the parse of one file and means nothing to another, so that set is computed per unit and the `declared` type names — which are strings — are the package's.
- **The polarity is held to rather than asserted.** Every test that shows a claim arriving has a twin showing it withheld: the same trait method whose neighbour genuinely pauses is still `NK1129`, `calls_reader` is still `Sync::No`, and a call that leaves the **package** is still unresolved — which is D1's half and step 3. Without the twins this change would be indistinguishable from relaxing the rule, and `open-work.md` names that as the one thing that must not be done here: it would trade a false refusal for a silent miscompilation.
- **Six tests in `crates/nikaia/tests/package_graph.rs`**, including the two-file program compiled and **run**, and one that the answer does not depend on the order the units arrive in — Part III 13.5 makes the ledger a pure function of the source tree and `--locked` compares it byte for byte, so the fixpoints walking a `BTreeMap` is a property to check rather than to argue.
- **`open-work.md`'s defect narrows rather than closes**, and its title moves with it: *when the call leaves the **package***. The two-package reproduction — `impl lib::Greeter for Fixed` whose body calls `lib::hello()` — is still refused, because a path dependency's ledger is still neither read nor written. Part III 13.5's **Status** note and the ADR index row move too; a stale Status note is a defect in its own right ([`docs/README.md`](docs/README.md) §1).

### Decided (a consumer reads a dependency's ledger)

- **[ADR-100](docs/specification/adr/adr-100.md).** A package's `nikaia.contracts` is written by the package's own build and read by every consumer, never derived on the consumer's side — [ADR-020](docs/specification/adr/adr-020.md) D5's rule for `std`, for every package. The inference graph is the **package**, not the file. The header gains a `[sources]` table with a SHA-256 per unit: a consumer believes a dependency while the hashes match, derives it again where they do not, and compares contracts byte for byte only under `--locked`. A build with a hundred unchanged dependencies hashes and infers nothing; a stale ledger is a hash that does not match, never a belief.
- **What it closes:** `open-work.md`'s *a `sync` body is refused as pausing when the call leaves the unit* — `NK1129` on a body that plainly does not pause — in both of its shapes, and `open-decisions.md`'s question of whether a consumer derives a dependency's contracts, which leaves that file. `NK1129` itself is unchanged.
- **Nothing of it is built**; `open-work.md` carries the five steps, the package-wide graph first because it needs no file-format change. *(Step 1 landed the same day — the entry above.)*

### Decided (the caller writes no `&`, and a review of the surface language)

- **[ADR-094](docs/specification/adr/adr-094.md).** Whether a parameter is lent or kept is the **callee's**: a view unless the body keeps the value, inferred into a `keeps` column beside `returns`, and the compiler writes the reference at the call the way it writes the `.await` and the `?`. A `for` lends, a `let` over a place is a view, and in-place change of a parameter is `mut` in the declaration, never at the call. `&T` in a declaration stays as the assertion, beside the inferred answer, as `sync` does. The one semantic cost — a kept value's cleanup point moves into the callee with nothing at the call — is accepted the way `close(self)` already is and narrated by the ledger diff. **Nothing of it is built**; `open-work.md` carries the five steps.
- **Counted, not argued:** 42 `&` at calls and loop heads in 913 non-comment lines of `examples/`, each repeating what the callee's signature had said; `for e in entries` followed by `entries.len()` type-checks clean and is refused below in Rust's words.
- **Withdrawn, not superseded** ([`docs/README.md`](docs/README.md) §2): [ADR-013](docs/specification/adr/adr-013.md) D7 carried two rows that put a `&` at a call (`fs::map(&path)`, `&totals.stations[a]`) and a sentence saying a value read after a call is passed as a view. They were a Stage 0 gap written up as a rule; the record now reads as though they were never there.
- **[`docs/language-review.md`](docs/language-review.md)** is the review this came out of: the surface language read as a newcomer would read it, every claim reproduced with a probe or quoted from the page — this, the catch-all `catch`, the two-line lost update, Part I 6.3 contradicting Part II 12.2 about `access`, `from` reserved against `std`'s own `rename(from:, to:)`, no `else if`, no list literal, no digit separators, and the order in which turning the language inside out would pay. One text type is [`open-decisions.md`](docs/open-decisions.md)'s fifth entry rather than part of ADR-094: a different axis and a larger change.

### Added (an `http` package, a program that reaches it, and what a package cannot yet take)

- **`examples/http/` is a Nikaia package and `examples/hello-http/` is a program that depends on it by a path.** It is the first place in the repository where the **Nikaia** arm of a path dependency is written down rather than described — the `type = "rust"` arm has had `examples/foreign-runtime/` all along. The two resolve differently and the manifest says so beside the line: a Nikaia path is read relative to the manifest's own directory, a `type = "rust"` path is handed to Cargo and read relative to the generated manifest under `target/`. That is why `foreign-runtime/serve` climbs four levels and this climbs one, and getting it wrong once is what made it worth a comment. `tests/project.rs` builds the real directory and checks the bytes, carriage returns included.
- **The guard that stops an example from escaping both lists only walked top-level `.nika` files**, so a *directory* escaped it entirely — and `examples/foreign-runtime/` had been escaping it since it was added. It walks directories now: one is known if some runnable entry reaches into it, or if it is listed with the test that checks it, and that test file has to exist.
- **What the package does not have is a server, and the reason is a language question rather than a socket.** `Server::route(path, handler)` cannot be *declared*: a parameter cannot be a function, because the type rule has a reference, a name, generic arguments and a `?` and nothing else — `fn apply(f: fn() -> String)` is a parse error at the `fn`. Meanwhile eight `std` ledger entries spell a parameter `f: fn(…)` and the corpus passes lambdas to all of them, so **the door exists and only `std` may walk through it**. That asymmetry is now `open-decisions.md`'s fourth entry, together with what a signature would have to say about code it is handed: `sync` has `from(f)`, `touches` deliberately has no such form and the ledger says why, and `throws` and `sharing` have never been asked. [ADR-029](docs/specification/adr/adr-029.md) D4's limit is what makes a server different from `sort_by_key` — `from` is for a lambda the callee runs before it returns, and a router *stores* one.
- **The other door is [ADR-078](docs/specification/adr/adr-078.md)'s `trait`, and it opens in one file and shuts at the package boundary.** A handler stored in a struct and dispatched through a bound compiles and runs today, in one file. Across a package: `[H: http::Handler]` is a parse error, because a bound is one name; `[H: Handler]` after `use http` is `NK1126`, because `use` brings nothing in and a bound has no way to say where the trait is. That is the decision. Two more things behind it are **defects**, and `open-work.md` §1 stops being empty one round after it said the next defect found would go there:
- **A trait a package publishes is implemented and then not callable.** The emitter writes `impl http::Handler for Fixed` and never brings the trait into scope, so `rustc` answers the user with *"items from traits can only be used if the trait is in scope; perhaps you want to import it"* — a [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md) message naming a rule the program has no way to satisfy, since there is no import to write. One file compiles, because there the trait is in the same module.
- **And a `sync` body is refused as pausing when the call crosses a package.** `NK1129` fires on a handler whose body calls the package it implements against — while the same build's own ledger records `sync = "inferred"` for both the callee and the method. Replacing the call with a struct literal makes it go away and the identical shape in one file compiles, so it is an **ordering** defect: the trait rule reads the column before the fixpoint has taken in another package's entries, and a callee absent from that fixpoint is read as one that pauses. `tests/traits.rs` already says these rules need the *finished* column; it builds one unit and cannot see this.
- **`fortunes.nika`'s entry said it waited on two runtime pieces and *"neither is a language question"*.** It has one, and this is it. Measured while checking: given a manifest that depends on `examples/http/`, the file stops before any of the three — `dsl postgres { … }` has no hole and `postgres` is not a grammar this compiler has — so the driver question comes first and the handler question is what it meets after that.

### Added (the two refusals that come with the lock's doors)

- **[ADR-099](docs/specification/adr/adr-099.md).** [ADR-039](docs/specification/adr/adr-039.md) D10 gave shared mutable state four doors and named the two mistakes that come with them. Part III Appendix C catalogued both and **nothing raised either** — which is the state `open-work.md` §2 calls the one that rots fastest: *a check with no program to be tested against is correctness that quietly stops being true*. The lock has had a type since [ADR-064](docs/specification/adr/adr-064.md) and Part II 12.2's counter runs at both settings, so these had something to be about and no reason left to wait.
- **`NK2204` names the door with the value in it.** `kasse = 42` looks like an ordinary assignment and is not: the lock has to be taken for the write. The repair is mechanical, so the help says to write `kasse.set(42)` rather than saying *"use a door"*, which would leave the reader to work out which of the four.
- **What cannot be quoted back is not quoted.** An expression has no span — [ADR-081](docs/specification/adr/adr-081.md) D2 gave one to `Binary` and to nothing else — so a literal, a name and a negation are rebuilt and anything else is an ellipsis. A help line that quoted the *wrong* thing would be worse than one that quotes nothing.
- **`NK2205` is syntactic on purpose**, which is D10's own line: the `get` written *inside* the `set`, on the **same** container by name. `kasse.set(kasse.get() + 100)` takes the lock twice and the value can change in between; reading one container while writing another takes each lock once and is an ordinary program; and the same pair spread over two lines is a question about what happened between two statements, which this rule does not ask.
- **These two, and not the other three the same entry lists.** `NK2201` and `NK2203` need to know what is *inside a door* and what a chain of calls reaches, and `NK2503` needs the reachability walk — two local rules beat five half-analysed ones, and the entry now says which three are left and why.
- **The walk over a `set`'s argument is the emitter's**, made `pub(crate)` rather than copied: a second walk over the same shape is a second thing to keep in step with the AST. Seven programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/lock_doors.rs`, including the corpus.

### Added (a `let` takes one name, or a flat tuple of them)

- **[ADR-098](docs/specification/adr/adr-098.md).** Part I 8.1.2 writes `let (user, rights, prefs) = overlap { … }` and Part II 12.5 writes `let (tx, rx) = channel::bounded(100)` — a form the specification uses twice, for two different constructs, and defines nowhere. `overlap { … }` **met** this rather than made it: the construct is built and was reached by its tuple in the meantime (`let r = overlap { … }`, then `r.0`), which works and reads worse than what the page promises. That line is a program now.
- **Not a pattern language.** `match` has patterns already and neither site needs them; what the two write is a tuple whose arity is known, taken apart by position.
- **One field on one variant rather than a second statement**, so all nineteen readers had to look — which is the point. **Three of them decline**, and that is the half worth recording: `contracts::order` goes **opaque** rather than reorder a statement that binds several names, `contracts::sharing` records no handle so each part stays at [ADR-037](docs/specification/adr/adr-037.md) D6's atomic floor, and `dsl` skips because a deferred block is one value. The same polarity three times — an analysis that cannot answer says nothing rather than guessing, and each fails safe: no reordering, no atomic taken away, no parameters claimed.
- **The widths are not compared**, deliberately: a value this compiler cannot see is `?`, and refusing a destructure because it *looks* like the wrong width would refuse a correct program ([Part III C.4](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)). A written type is refused as `NK1136` rather than ignored, and a **nested** tuple gets a sentence rather than a list of tokens — with a test that an unrelated unbalanced bracket does *not* get that sentence.
- **Seven programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/tuple_let.rs`**, including the shape compiled and run, and a part answering for the type it was taken from. **And the specification sweep moved twice, both forwards**: Part I 8.1.2's own line stopped being a *fragment* and became a body the checker reads, and Part II 12.5's `channel::bounded` block now **lowers**.
- **And it turned up `_`.** Checking whether to refuse a `_` in the tuple form found that the single-name form has been accepting it all along: `let _ = f()` compiles and lowers to Rust's `let _`, which **discards** rather than binds — a value bound to a name is dropped at the end of its scope, and one bound to Rust's `_` is dropped immediately. For a lock guard that is a different program. Nothing in the tree writes one, so it is filed as upkeep with the decision it needs, rather than narrowed here.
- **Two false sentences in the postfix-`??` entry, corrected rather than deleted**: it said Part I 2.3's nullable types are themselves a parse error, and `let a: i64? = 3` lowers and runs. That claim was the reason given for the entry being upkeep, and the reason holds for a different one.

### Added (a `comptime` binding where an item stands)

- **[ADR-097](docs/specification/adr/adr-097.md).** [ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md) D2 decided **both** places and built one: `comptime MAX = 1000` at the top of a file was a parse error while the same line inside a body parsed, folded and ran. Part I 9.2 already lists **Constants** among the items `pub` applies to, so the rule for a constant another package may read was written and the syntax for one was not.
- **`Item::Comptime` had been in the AST since that record**, with its `public` field, and nothing produced or consumed it — a variant nothing reaches is a decision that looks built from the inside.
- **What the item form needed and the body form did not is a frame under every body, filled before the item loop.** Both halves are load-bearing: *before*, because a function declared **above** a constant sees it as much as one below — an item is visible in its whole scope, and that is what makes it an item; and *never popped*, because this checker's scope is a stack pushed per function and there is nothing under all of them otherwise. That half has a test of its own, because a pass that filled the frame as it walked would pass every other test here.
- **One function under both places**, since D2's own sentence is that the difference is *"where the name is visible, never what may stand to the right of the `=`"*. The fold, `NK1127`, the declared-type check and the spelling the emitter is handed are one thing in one place — and a place where `NK1127` did **not** fire would be a place where `comptime` quietly means `let`, which is the promise the word exists for.
- **Two lines move in the error corpus**, both correct: `comptime` now appears in the list of what may stand where an item does, which is a parse error's note getting truer.
- **Five programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/comptime_item.rs`**, including the whole shape compiled and **run** — four kinds of initialiser, `pub`, and a function reading a constant declared below it.

### Changed (`examples/foreign-runtime/` explained the boundary with a rule that is gone)

- **Four programs, three manifests, a shim's doc comment and `docs/foreign-runtime.md` itself** all said `Shared` lowers to an `Rc` at `user_parallelism = no` ([ADR-037](docs/specification/adr/adr-037.md) D3) and that `Shared` was unbuilt. D6 narrowed D3 — `Shared` is an **atomic** count at both settings — and [ADR-064](docs/specification/adr/adr-064.md) built it. This is where a reader goes to learn what crossing means, so a stale explanation here is worth more than its size.
- **The notes page had also predicted a failure mode that D6 then made impossible**: *"once `Shared` is built, the same source will compile at `yes` and fail at `no`"*. There is no `yes`/`no` split left to be inconsistent about, and D6's own argument is that paragraph's reached from the other side. The correction is kept **beside** the prediction rather than written over it, because what that page is for is the finding and the finding includes how it was reasoned.
- **What the four still demonstrate is sharper than what they claimed.** A Nikaia `Shared` cannot be written into this shape at all now — [ADR-061](docs/specification/adr/adr-061.md) D1 refuses one handed to code nothing describes, since D7's per-value inference makes a `Shared[Conn]` an `Rc` for one value and an `Arc` for another in the same program and no foreign signature can name both. So `shim`'s `LocalHandle` stopped being a stand-in for something unbuilt and became the only way to build the shape: the question is answered with a value that exists rather than a stand-in for one.

### Fixed (a type nothing declares is refused rather than lowered)

- **[ADR-096](docs/specification/adr/adr-096.md).** A **value** nothing declares has had `NK1117` since [ADR-051](docs/specification/adr/adr-051.md) — *"nothing declares `q`"*. A **type** had nothing, so `let x: Widgit = 3` lowered verbatim and came back as `rustc`'s *"cannot find type `Widgit` in this scope"*, about a file nobody wrote. [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)'s rule held for one half of this language's names and not the other, and [ADR-064](docs/specification/adr/adr-064.md) had fixed one such name by **building** it while leaving the class untouched.
- **The known set is derived, not listed**, and that is what makes it answerable without refusing correct programs: `OFFERED` is Part I 2.2's own set and already answers what `as` may name; the ledger's `types` map is every type this program and `std` declare, read under both the qualified key and its last segment; and the declaration around the name contributes its own type parameters. What is left is a short table of names the *emitter* writes that no ledger declares.
- **A qualified name is left alone.** `http::Response` names a package's type, and whether this build can see that package is [ADR-046](docs/specification/adr/adr-046.md) D2's question with a message of its own — saying *"nothing declares it"* about a name a dependency does declare would be [Part III C.4](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md).
- **Ten positions, because a check that reads one of them is one somebody routes around without meaning to**: a parameter, a result, a struct field, an enum variant's payload in both shapes, a trait method's signature, an `impl` method's, a `let`'s annotation, and every argument nested inside any of those.
- **The measurement is the point, and it found two real things.** `examples/fortunes.nika` wrote `List[Fortune]` — a type name this language does not have; Part I 2.2's prose calls the concept *List (Vector)* and every program writes `Vec`, which is what `std`'s `ListExt` is implemented for. The file never built for another reason and so never said so. And **five specification blocks stopped lowering, every one of which was already `error[E0425]` below** — *"cannot find … in this scope"*, from `rustc`, about the generated file. The refusal moved from the backend into this compiler, which is the whole of what C.1 asks for.
- **The sweep's floor moves down for the first time, 52 to 48, with its reason written beside it**: these are not programs turned back into fragments, they are fragments the compiler had been accepting because it could not read a type name. They are three different things, and only the first is the sweep's own doing — `impl User` and `-> Config` declare their type in a *neighbouring* block, which the sweep compiles separately; `NotFound(Path)` and `-> List[Row]` name types the specification writes and `std` does not publish, which is `fortunes.nika`'s finding arrived at from the page's side; and `fn total(p: &Player)` names a type the specification declares **nowhere at all**, as do `Object` and `SqlParser`.
- **And a fixture had been standing on the absence this closes.** `a_field_of_an_unknown_type_says_nothing` wrote `&Row`, a type nothing declares, to mean *a type the checker cannot see*. What it is actually about is the **fields**, so its receiver is now a type `std` publishes whose fields its ledger does not record — the type resolves, the field cannot be looked up, and nothing is claimed about it.

### Fixed (a loop that cannot end needs no unreachable `return`)

- **[ADR-093](docs/specification/adr/adr-093.md).** [ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md) D3 decided it and named it the one real cost of that record's *no second keyword*: `fn forever() -> i32 { while true { … } }` was `NK1104`, so an accept loop, an event loop or a supervisor had to end with a `return 0` nobody reaches — and a reader of that line cannot tell dead code from a mistake.
- **It could not have been built before [ADR-085](docs/specification/adr/adr-085.md), whatever the checker did.** `while true { }` is `()` in the language below and `loop { }` diverges and is `!`, so the refusal would only have moved from the checker to `rustc`, about a file nobody wrote. That record emits `loop`, so the checker now claims divergence in exactly the cases the emitter delivers it — the two halves agree by construction rather than by agreement.
- **The test is two halves, and the second is [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md)'s**: the literal `true`, **and** no `break` bound to this loop. This entry's own earlier version had argued the work was small *because* `break` and `continue` did not exist — *"the analysis other languages need for this question is, here, one test on the condition"*. That was true and is not, which is the kind of sentence that stays quoted long after its premise leaves.
- **The walk over-approximates *a jump leaves this loop*, on purpose.** It descends into a lambda, where a `break` is not bound to this loop at all. The two failure modes are not symmetric: a false *"a jump leaves it"* asks for the unreachable `return` this record removes, which is where every program already is, while a false *"nothing leaves it"* lets a body fall off its end and hands `rustc` a file nobody wrote.
- **Four programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/never_ends.rs`**, including the emitted `loop` handed to `rustc` and **run** — which is what makes the claim safe rather than merely quiet. The two shapes that look like it and are not — a condition that is a name, and a `while true` a `break` leaves — still answer for their declared type.
- **Seven citations moved with it.** `open-work.md`'s numbers renumber when an entry closes, and `§2.12` was named by two ADRs, two index rows, a notes page and an emitter comment. All seven now cite [ADR-093](docs/specification/adr/adr-093.md), which does not move.

### Fixed (a trait a package publishes is implemented and then callable)

- **[ADR-095](docs/specification/adr/adr-095.md).** Rust needs a trait **in scope** before its methods can be called, and `impl http::Handler for Fixed` does not put it there — so a trait a package publishes could be implemented and then not called. [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)'s class twice over: the backend's words about a file nobody wrote, and the remedy the message names — *"perhaps you want to import it"* — is one the **program cannot write**, because [ADR-046](docs/specification/adr/adr-046.md) D2 gives this language no import at all.
- **The same shape in one file compiles**, which is what said this was one missing emitted line rather than a question about the language. `tests/traits.rs` builds one unit and could never have seen it.
- **Qualified is the whole of the test.** A `::` in the trait's name is exactly what says the declaration is in another crate, so a trait of this program's own and a trait the compiler reads rather than a `.nika` file wrote (`impl Error for ConfigError`, [ADR-023](docs/specification/adr/adr-023.md) D3) are both left alone without the test needing to know about either.
- **A real two-package project that builds and runs**, in `tests/project.rs`, printing what the handler returned — and failing with the message above when the loop is removed. Its `impl` body is a struct literal rather than a call into the package, and that is not tidiness: a call out of the unit is `NK1129` today, so a fixture that made one would be testing two things and failing for the other.

### Changed (the `sync` defect is a unit boundary, not a package one)

- **Filed as a package boundary; measured as a unit one.** The shorter reproduction is two files of **one** package with no packages in sight — a trait method calling a function in the file next door is `NK1129`. `Ledger::infer` runs once per **unit**, so `sync::infer`'s graph is that one file's; a callee outside it is in neither `own` nor `std` and `reach_of` blocks on it. `Sync::No`'s own documentation says it means *"can pause **or** could not be vouched for"*, and every reader takes the first meaning.
- **The obvious repair was built and reverted**, which is why the entry now says what it costs rather than what it looked like. A settling pass — re-running the assembly with the previous round's ledger as the library until it stops changing — fixes both reproductions and breaks the three-package test: with `app → http → deeper`, `http`'s own build settles and emits `ok` as a plain `fn` while `app`'s build cannot see `deeper` and still writes an `.await`. They agree today only because both are equally ignorant.
- **That is not a bug in the pass**; it is [ADR-053](docs/specification/adr/adr-053.md) D3 working as decided — a transitive package is deliberately not in this program's ledger, so a consumer **cannot** derive what a dependency derived about itself. Which of the two stops being derived is [ADR-100](docs/specification/adr/adr-100.md), and the answer is the first: the consumer reads the dependency's published ledger ([ADR-020](docs/specification/adr/adr-020.md)'s own purpose), or the program's ledger becomes transitive and D3 reopens.
- **What must not be done meanwhile is relax `NK1129`.** It is right about what it reads; what it reads conflates two facts, and making it quieter would trade a false refusal for a silent miscompilation — the worse half of `docs/README.md`'s list.

### Fixed (the superseded grammar entry, and three records whose decisions were not citable)

- **`dsl … from …` is superseded and the replacement is unbuilt** ([ADR-082](docs/specification/adr/adr-082.md) D1: `Json.value(input)`), which is why the checker still knows only the old form — asked about, and worth writing down rather than leaving as a surprise in a comment. [ADR-091](docs/specification/adr/adr-091.md) D4's line now says it is **on borrowed time**: when the entry is a call it is answered from a contract like every other call, and the line is *deleted* rather than edited. The thing that has to travel with the migration is named: **a generated entry rule must carry a `throws`**, or `NK1134` refuses all eight programs that write `catch` beside an entry.
- **ADR-082 rested on a fact that had stopped being true.** Its §5 gave *"no program in the tree writes it"* as the reason to **remove** the old form rather than deprecate it. Counted while the new refusal ran over the corpus: **eight programs write it on nine lines** — `1brc`, `access-log`, `calc`, `config`, `inventory/stock`, `json`, `k-nucleotide`, `report` — plus Part II 10.2 and three test files. The record now carries the count; whether removal is still right is a decision and is now `open-decisions.md`'s third entry, with both answers costed — remove-and-migrate-together is unseparable, deprecate-then-remove leaves a window with two spellings for one thing, which is the defect ADR-082 §1 names.
- **And it had no entry on the work list at all**, only a subordinate clause in the build-time-evaluator entry — which is the exact pattern that entry itself was written about, one round earlier. It has one now, carrying the defect it closes: the emitter picks a grammar's entry rule by source order with a `par_fold` one winning, so **a grammar with two `pub` rules gets one of them silently**.
- **Two more stale citations, found by adding an entry rather than closing one.** Inserting the new §2.14 moved everything below it, and the two sentences pointing into that range turned out to have been wrong *before* the insertion — `adr-077.md` said §2.14 for the item form, `adr/README.md` said §2.13 for what a `const` waits on. The renumber is only what made anybody look. Both now name their subject, and `open-work.md`'s upkeep entry counts eight.
- **Three of this round's own records had decisions nothing could cite.** `check-adr-refs.py` reads a decision from a `### Dn` heading; ADR-090, 091 and 092 wrote theirs as bold paragraphs, so every `Dn` in them was dangling and nothing said so until a sentence cited one. All fourteen are headings now, and the first citation of `ADR-091 D4` is what surfaced it — the gate works, it just cannot fire until somebody points at the hole.

### Fixed (a grammar fold's `init`, `step` and `merge` are code)

- **[ADR-092](docs/specification/adr/adr-092.md).** A fold's three lambdas are expressions inside a **pattern**, and the checker's grammar walk takes a rule's *action block* and nothing else — so `fold(N, zero, fn(acc, m) { nothing_declares_this })` lowered without a word and `|acc, m| { nothing_declares_this }` reached `rustc`, which answered about a file nobody wrote. The same walk is what refuses an undeclared name everywhere else in the language.
- **It had been sitting behind a question rather than behind work.** [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md) D4 closed the half a **jump** can reach and deliberately stopped, because walking those bodies with the whole checker *"would newly refuse programs for reasons that have nothing to do with the construct that found this"*. That is a thing to measure: it refuses **nothing**. `examples/1brc.nika` and `examples/access-log.nika` both write `par_fold(RULE, Type::new, fn(acc, m) { acc.record(m) }, Type::merge)` — a path as a value in two positions and a method on a `?` in the third — and all of it goes through untouched.
- **So D4's own walk is deleted rather than kept beside the new one.** `Expr::Closure`'s arm already crosses a boundary and counts loops from zero, so a jump in a step meets `NK1132` through the path every other lambda's does; with the ordinary walk in place, the compiler reported the old function as dead code. Two walks over one construct are two chances to disagree.
- **And the jump's message improves in the bargain**, which is the argument for doing it this way round: the old walk ran with no barrier set, so it said *"`break` … and this is not in a loop"* and offered `return` as the way out — wrong advice inside a grammar action. It now says *"the nearest loop is outside this lambda"*, and the help is to hand a `bool` back. [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md) D6's backstop in the **lowering** is untouched: a walk cannot answer whether it is complete, and the emitter's refusal does not have to.
- **The frame is load-bearing and was measured before it was written.** A fold can stand beside a binding in a sequence — `head:N rest:fold(N, zero, fn(acc, m) { acc + m + head })` parses — so the walk runs inside the frame the rule's pattern makes. Without it, `head` is `NK1117`. It runs *outside* `expected`, because what a fold's `init` and `step` build is the parser backend's running value on the way to the rule's type, not the type itself.
- **What those lambdas now get is every rule the language has**, because they go through the same walk — `NK1117`, `NK1132`, `NK1128`, and whatever is added next. None of it had to be written for the construct.
- **Seven programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/fold_lambdas.rs`**, and the corpus sweep among them asserts it found files, so a corpus gone quiet cannot pass by checking nothing.
- **`docs/open-work.md` §1 is empty for the first time** — which the page now says is a statement about what to do next rather than a victory lap: the sections below are decided-and-unbuilt and upkeep, and neither outranks a defect. Three defects closed this round, and every one of them was found by **running a program** — the specification's, the corpus's, or one written while building a fixture for something else.

### Fixed (a `catch` over an expression that cannot fail is refused)

- **[ADR-091](docs/specification/adr/adr-091.md).** The lowering makes a `catch` a `match` over a `Result`, so `text.len() as i64 catch { 1000 }` produced `E0308` about the generated file, naming a `match` and an `Ok` arm the author never wrote — [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)'s class. Found while building a fixture for [ADR-090](docs/specification/adr/adr-090.md), which is why the reproduction is that record's own opening line: the `catch` there was written to ignore its error and turned out to have none to ignore.
- **The question was already being asked, from the other end.** [ADR-025](docs/specification/adr/adr-025.md) D1's `NK2605` refuses a call that can fail where nothing handles it, and its rule is that *the ledger is the fact* — reported only where a contract **says** so, and silent about a callee nobody has written down. `NK1134` is that standard read backwards.
- **Two answers and not one, because [Part III C.4](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md) makes only the first actionable.** The walk records *something here can fail* and *something here could not be looked up* separately: a call no contract describes says **nothing** about whether it throws, and letting that read as *it cannot* would refuse programs that are right. The refusal fires on **known not to fail**, never on *not known to fail*.
- **The corpus is what proved that is not theoretical.** Six examples — `access-log`, `calc`, `config`, `json`, `k-nucleotide`, `report` — were refused the first time this ran over `examples/`, all at once: each writes `dsl … from … catch { … }`, which can fail by [ADR-023](docs/specification/adr/adr-023.md) D9 and carries no contract to say so, because a grammar run is not a call. A `dsl` template block and an `asm` block answer *no answer* for the same reason from the other side — what they become is the emitter's business, not this walk's.
- **Refused rather than dropped, and that is the decision rather than the smaller change.** Emitting the expression without the `match` would take the program the author wrote and quietly delete a block from it — including a `return` inside the handler, which [ADR-034](docs/specification/adr/adr-034.md) makes the *function's* return. A handler that can never run is a belief about the program, and a belief that is wrong is worth a sentence.
- **One hole, deliberate and written down.** `(text.parse() catch { 1 }) catch { 2 }` really does have nothing left for the second handler, and is **not** refused: saying so needs to know what a handler's own failure does, which is [ADR-034](docs/specification/adr/adr-034.md)'s question. The outer guard is told *no answer* instead — the direction that cannot refuse a program that is right.
- **Six programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/infallible_catch.rs`**, two of which fail if the `dsl` line is removed: the reproduction, four shapes refused, three left alone, the nested `catch`, a grammar run, and a sweep of every `.nika` in `examples/` and `tests/samples/`.

### Fixed (a `catch` that ignores the error binds `_error`)

- **[ADR-090](docs/specification/adr/adr-090.md).** Kap 7.1 gives the failure the name `error` whether or not the handler reads one, so `catch { 1000 }` — the shape Part I 7.1 teaches first — reached the author as `warning: unused variable: error`, about a binding that exists nowhere in their program. [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)'s class one severity down, and the second half of what [ADR-074](docs/specification/adr/adr-074.md)'s survey turned up.
- **The question was already being asked, for something else.** `contracts::order::names_in_block` collects every name a block mentions and runs over these very blocks for the ordering analysis; `block_mentions` is a public question over it. No second walk, and no second notion of *"is this name read"* — which matters, because a second notion is a second thing that can disagree.
- **Its over-approximation is what makes it usable, and the reasoning is the whole decision.** The walk counts a word inside an interpolated string's raw text, so `f"{error}"` is a mention; it counts `errors` as `errors` and not as `error`, which was checked. The two ways of being wrong are **not symmetric**: a false *"mentioned"* writes `error` and lands exactly where yesterday already was, while a false *"not mentioned"* writes `_error` under a handler that reads it and does not compile. Only the second is a new failure, and it is the one an over-approximate walk cannot reach — so a walk that has to *lean* does work a precise analysis would have to be *right* for.
- **One place spells the name.** `emit::CAUGHT` is what the emitter writes and what the walk is asked about; two spellings would be a binding that goes unread while the walk looks for the other, which is this defect arrived at from the other side.
- **Not an `allow`**, for [ADR-074](docs/specification/adr/adr-074.md) D3's reason: the warning is `rustc` reading the emitted code correctly, so the emitted code is the thing to change. An `allow` would also be silent the day the warning is about something real.
- **Six programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/catch_handler.rs`**, and two of them fail if the arm is reverted: a table of four handler shapes against the arm each must produce, a mention nested inside an `if`, the `n-body` line compiled by `rustc` with its stderr read for the word `unused`, and a handler that *does* read the error compiled and **run** — so the `_error` direction is checked by a program that would not build if it were taken wrongly.
- **And the fixture that found the next one.** The reproduction's own opening line, `text.len() as i64 catch { 1000 }`, cannot fail: the `catch` lowers to a `match` over something that is not a `Result` and gets `E0308` about the generated file. Same class, different defect, and it is filed rather than fixed — `open-work.md` carries it as *a `catch` on an expression that cannot fail does not compile*, with the polarity that decides it written down: refuse on **known not to throw**, never on *not known to throw*, because an unresolved call says nothing and refusing it would be [Part III C.4](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md).

### Fixed (six citations named an entry by its number and meant another one)

- **Found by reading, in the same round.** `open-work.md`'s head says to cite an entry by its **subject** because the numbers move when something closes. Six live sentences cited a number: three said *"§1.1"* about a generic function lowering without its `<T>` (which it has done since [ADR-074](docs/specification/adr/adr-074.md)), one said it about a trait whose method pauses (`NK1129` since [ADR-080](docs/specification/adr/adr-080.md)), one said the qualified constructor path is a parse error (`http::Response(status: 400)` parses and lowers), and one pointed at a `§3.7` that does not exist.
- **Two of the six were not merely misnumbered but false**, and nothing could have caught them: a citation into a notes page is the one kind that cannot be checked mechanically, because the entry it points at exists — it is simply a different entry. `check-adr-refs.py` covers ADR numbers and has nothing to say here, and the guard for this shape was [measured and not built](docs/specification/adr/README.md#reserved-numbers) because it produces a fifth false alarm.
- **All six now name a record or a subject**, and `open-work.md` carries the list as its own entry, so the next reader finds the evidence that the rule has to be applied rather than merely written.

### Added (the build-time evaluator is on the work list, as two entries)

- **Three records were waiting on a piece of work that `open-work.md` did not mention.** [ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md) D5 wants a **call** in a `comptime` initialiser, [ADR-079](docs/specification/adr/adr-079.md) §3 wants a **loop and `push`** to build a table, and [ADR-088](docs/specification/adr/adr-088.md) D1 wants a **loop over a type's fields**. Each named it in a subordinate clause; none of them is the list people read to find something to do. §2.14 now is.
- **What exists today, counted:** `comptime`'s evaluator is `fold.rs`, **124 lines**, and it knows an integer literal, a name that already folded, a negation, and `+ - * / %`. No call, no loop, no text, no aggregate. What bounds the work is already decided — [ADR-075](docs/specification/adr/adr-075.md) D1 and D2 — so this is an interpreter for a **restricted** language and what it must refuse is written down rather than invented on the way.
- **And it is two entries, not one, because conflating them makes it look like re-implementing `winnow-grammar`.** §2.15 is the other half and the reason is not effort: running a grammar at build time by **interpreting** it would be a second implementation of the same semantics, and Part II 10.2 promises that one grammar means the same thing at both stages. With two implementations that stops being a property and becomes a hope — and the disagreements would land in implicit whitespace, repetition bounds, the commit point, frames, interning and spans, presenting as *"this file parsed while the program was built and fails while it runs"*, for the same file and the same grammar.
- **There is nothing to borrow, either.** `winnow-grammar` is a code *generator*: its model crate parses, validates and analyses the grammar language and hands the result to a macro that writes a parser — *"intended to be used by procedural macros that generate parsers"*. It contains no interpreter.
- **So §2.15 is compile-and-run, not interpretation**: build the *generated* parser during the build and run it, so the agreement between the two stages is a tautology rather than a claim. The second compilation is the cost [ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) Q4 named, and [ADR-021](docs/specification/adr/adr-021.md)'s cache turns it from *every build* into *when the grammar changes*. No new security model: a grammar's actions are Nikaia, and [ADR-075](docs/specification/adr/adr-075.md) already covers what a build-time body may do.

### Fixed (a `??`'s fallback is one value, or an expression in brackets)

- **[ADR-089](docs/specification/adr/adr-089.md).** `??` sits above the whole binary chain, so its fallback reached rightwards across every operator there is: `a ?? 0 > 3` was `a ?? (0 > 3)` while looking like `(a ?? 0) > 3`.
- **It was filed as a suspicion, with the exact question that would decide it** — *is there a shape where both readings type-check, so the program silently takes the other one* — and the answer is yes. With `a: bool? = false`, `x: bool = true`, `y: bool = false`, the line `a ?? x == y` is **`false`** and `(a ?? x) == y` is **`true`**. Both are well typed, so nothing downstream would have caught it: a silent wrong value, which is the worst thing in `docs/README.md`'s list.
- **D1: the fallback takes `unary_expr`.** A literal, a name, a call, a field, a `-1` and a bracketed expression reach it; no binary operator does. The chain survives, because the rule recurses on its own tail ([ADR-066](docs/specification/adr/adr-066.md) D4).
- **Refused rather than re-precedenced, and that is the decision rather than the cheap way out.** The alternative is to raise `??` above comparison so the line means what it looks like — which is what Swift does, while C# and Kotlin read it the way this language did. **The languages disagree**, so whichever precedence is chosen somebody reads the line wrong and gets no message at all; making the author say which they meant is the only answer right for both readers. And the polarity settles the rest: a narrowing can be relaxed into a precedence later, a precedence cannot be un-chosen. It costs nothing today — every `??` in `examples/` has a fallback that is a literal, a name or a call.
- **D2: the refusal says why, from what a reader sees.** A grammar refusal cannot always say why — this one fails *after* the fallback has succeeded on `0`, so the backend's message is about an enclosing rule's brace and the caret lands on a token nobody typed wrong. A note is added when the rendered message shows a `??` to the left of the caret and an operator at it, naming both readings. The offending token is deliberately **not** quoted back: the backend reports the first character it could not use, so a `==` arrives as `=` and a help echoing it would read `(a ?? 0) = …`.

### Changed (there is no macro system)

- **[ADR-088](docs/specification/adr/adr-088.md)** withdraws the design of Part II 10.3 — `macro`, code written as data, and something attached to a declaration — and keeps the **problem** it names: generating code from a type's shape. That needs none of the three, because `comptime` plus a **bound** is enough. Before reserving the words, the question was put the other way round: *what does this language actually need, now that it has `comptime`?*
- **Zig and D are why.** Zig has no macro system at all, and its version of 10.3's own example is an ordinary function: `inline for` over `@typeInfo(T).Struct.fields`. The absence is deliberate there, on a readability argument — nothing is generated behind the reader's back. D went the other way and shows what not to take: `mixin` compiles a **string** built at build time as if it were source, which is maximum power and the best-known readability complaint about the language — and is [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)'s class, messages pointing at text nobody wrote.
- **D2, and it is the part that is better than the model:** a type's shape is reached as **members**, and the **bound** is what makes them exist. `fn describe[T: Struct](value: T)` — `T: Struct` is what makes `T.fields` exist, the way any bound makes a method exist ([ADR-078](docs/specification/adr/adr-078.md)), so the trait system does the navigation Zig does with a union tag. No `@typeInfo`, no builtin at all, and `@field(v, f.name)` becomes `field.of(value)`, a method rather than syntax.
- **D3: a bound, not an untyped parameter.** Zig's `anytype` is the most-named weakness of its build-time system — the signature says nothing, so a caller learns its type does not fit only when something deep in the body fails, with the message pointing into somebody else's code. The bound moves one whole class of failure to the call site and leaves inside only what is genuinely per-field.
- **D4: one loop, and no second keyword** — derived rather than agreed: `T.fields` is known while the program is built, so a loop over it *cannot* be anything but unrolled. The record states why this is not the fault [ADR-082](docs/specification/adr/adr-082.md) removed: there the **operand** changed meaning with its position; here the loop means what it always means and only the **stage** differs.
- **D5: the body is checked once per unrolled turn.** `field.of(value)` is a `String` for one field and an `i32` for the next, so there is no single type to check it against once. **At run time this costs nothing** — what is left is the calls somebody would have written by hand. At build time it is fields × the types actually used, and an ordinary loop over a run-time list is checked once, as always. The cost that matters is the **message**, which must name the unrolling: `= unrolling T.fields for User, at field avatar`. Without that line this is the error class C++ templates carried for twenty years.
- **D6: what cannot be read is asked, not spelled.** Every build-time system shares the problem that you cannot see what a function becomes for a given type; the usual answer is to invent syntax. This project has the other one already — `--overlaps`, `--sharing`, `--trust` — so `--comptime` prints what was unrolled, for the types actually used.
- **D7: `macro`, `quote` and `with` are reserved and are not constructs.** 35 words become 38. Reserved *although* the language does not want them, on [ADR-071](docs/specification/adr/adr-071.md) D3's polarity — un-reserving is the free direction — and because all three are words a reader from another language will type, where a reserved word can carry a message and a name cannot.
- **What is named and not decided:** capturing an **expression**. Reflection describes types; a LINQ-shaped query needs `u.age > 18` as a tree for a provider to translate. It is the one capability left that would be genuinely new, and the record's job was to make sure it is the only one rather than one of four.

### Fixed (three findings the change turned up in other people's work)

- **`macro` left the escape sweep.** `tests/reserved_below.rs` walks the words Rust reserves and Nikaia does not, checking each is escaped rather than refused — and `macro` was one of them. It moves to that test's own `RESERVED_HERE_TOO`, beside `trait`, which is the mechanism the test was built with. It stays in the emitter's list, because that list says what the language *below* reserves, which is still true.
- **A typecheck fixture used `quote` as an ordinary name**, which was the point: `let q = quote { 1 + 1 }` parsed as a name beside a block and lowered in silence, and `NK1117` was what caught it. The grammar catches it now, earlier and better, so the fixture uses an ordinary name and the comment says why.
- **Part I 2.1's table had drifted by four words** — it was missing `trait` as well as the three added here. It is regenerated from `RESERVED_WORDS` and compared mechanically: 38 against 38.
- **The consistency pass found one thing in my own text.** The status note said *what is built is the bound*, which reads as though `[T: Struct]` works. Running the example says something earlier and sharper: `error[NK1117]: nothing declares T` — because **a type is not a value here**, so `T.fields` reads `T` where a value stands. That is the deepest of three missing pieces and it was not in the list; the second is that `Struct` is not a trait anything declares, and only the third is the build-time loop. The note now carries the compiler's own output, and says the bound *mechanism* is built while this particular bound does not exist.
- Also carried along: Chapter 10's opening promised AST manipulation, 10.5 opened by contrasting itself with `quote`, and `docs/spec-promises.md` had three rows and an open question about whether these pages should exist — the question is now answered, and it was a removal.

### Fixed (one statement, two calls, one wrap between them)

- **Part I 2.3's `Some(…)` was keyed by the parameter and not by the call.** `let r = pick(1) + pick(null)` came out `pick(Some(1)) + pick(Some(None))` — a double wrap around a value that already *was* `None`, which `rustc` refuses about a file nobody wrote (Part III C.1). The key was *(statement, callee as written, argument position)*, and the checker's own doc comment stated the design in the words that contain the bug: *"a statement may hold several calls, and one call may pass several arguments — so the callee's written name and the index together say which."* They say which **parameter**. A statement that calls one function twice had one entry for two arguments, and the last one walked won — so either argument could get the other's answer, depending on the order they were written in.
- **The same design, the same defect, in a struct literal**, found by asking the next obvious question: `hold(P { x: 2 }) + hold(P { x: null })` was `P { x: Some(2) }, P { x: Some(None) }`. A statement may build two literals, and the key was *(statement, field name)*.
- **The fix is a key that says which call**, and what it is made of is decided by what fails: an argument has **no span** — which is the reason the side table exists at all; the **address** of the expression is stable for a statement walked twice and not for an `f"…"` hole, which is *text* until each pass parses a tree of its own; and the **order** the calls are walked in would make the two passes agree by assumption rather than by construction. So it is structural — the argument itself, rendered the same from either pass, with the shared interner making an identifier render identically even in a re-parsed hole. Two textually identical arguments share an entry and want the same answer.
