The struct key also gains the **type**, because `P { x }` and `Q { x }` in one statement are two shorthand fields the value cannot tell apart.
- **It costs nothing on the path every argument takes**: the outer key is looked up exactly as before, and the shape is built only when that lookup finds something — which happens only where a wrap was recorded for this statement, callee and position.
- **Six tests, each of which fails without the fix** — the two orders, the two holes, the two literals, the two structs, and one that runs the program and checks the arithmetic (`1 + 7` and `2 + 9`).
- **And the entry that filed it was wrong about it.** [ADR-087](docs/specification/adr/adr-087.md) §3 reported it as *"a `null` in the second hole of an `f"…"`"* — where it was met, not what it was — and gave a condition (*"two holes with a non-`null` in the first"*) that a probe two lines longer would have replaced with the real one. The record stands as written and [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) carries the correction beside the closed entry, because **a narrow characterisation is a guess about the cause wearing the clothes of a reproduction**, and the guess was what made it look like a string problem for three commits.

### Added (a head holds every expression)

- **[ADR-087](docs/specification/adr/adr-087.md).** [ADR-086](docs/specification/adr/adr-086.md) D3 wrote the rule — *a head parses the same language as a body, minus exactly the forms a `{` begins* — and its D4 immediately made an exception to it, leaving `??` out because no program had run into it. Asked what **else** the head refused, the answer was not one level: `a ?? 0`, `a as i64`, `null` and `(a, b)` were each a parse error in the position Part I 3.2 describes with no restriction at all.
- **`null` is what says which kind of defect that was.** It is not an operator or a precedence level; it is a **literal**, in a head that already took `false`. A head that takes one and refuses the other is not a restriction anybody could deduce — it is a list that was copied and came up short.
- **So the test changes, and that is the decision** (D3): **a reproduction is the right evidence for adding a construct and the wrong evidence for an inconsistency in where an existing one may stand.** The first question is *should this exist* and has a cost to weigh; the second is *does this already exist everywhere it should*, and waiting on it does not gather evidence — it just means the next person meets it, and there is nothing to learn from their meeting it that is not already known.
- **The chain is now the ordinary one, level for level**: `??`, range, `||`, `&&`, comparison, `+`, `*`, `as`, unary, postfix, primary — with `null`, a tuple and `dsl … from …` among the primaries. What `head_primary` drops is a list short enough to write out: `struct_lit`, `block_expr`, `if_expr`, `match_expr`, `overlap_expr`, a `dsl … { … } eod`, and `spawn`, whose lambda is a brace.
- **And those are excluded from a *spelling*, not from the position** (D2): `if p == (P { x: 1 }) { … }` parses, and did before. `paren_expr` takes a whole `expr`, so the restriction stops at the outermost token. It is not *"these constructs are unavailable in a condition"* but *"the token that opens a body is reserved for the body"* — a syntactic necessity with a one-character way round it, which Part I 3.2 now shows instead of listing operators.
- **Where a rare alternative sits is a cost every common one in front of it pays** (D4), and both placements were measured rather than copied. `dsl … from …` goes **last**: at the front it was a failed keyword match ahead of every primary in every head, and it took the headline of a parse error with it — `if { }` answered *"expected one of `!`, `-`, `dsl`, `false`, `true`, identifier"*, leading with the rarest of the six. `null` goes after the literals rather than beside `false`, worth **0.45 %** of a compile on a head-dense file.
- **Cost** (D5): **5,016 instructions per head** — double [ADR-086](docs/specification/adr/adr-086.md)'s 2,536 for double the additions, and higher than the level count suggests because `head_cast` and the new primaries are tried **per operand**, not per head. **+0.5 % to +1.2 %** of a compile on real programs. Accepted for D3's reason: what is bought is not four constructs but the end of a class of surprise.
- **The test that matters does not test the four constructs.** `a_head_parses_what_a_body_parses` lowers the same expression in both positions and compares the two lowerings, so a level that mirrors its counterpart *badly* fails as readily as one that is missing. It caught this record's own first draft, which assumed `a ?? 0 > 3` is `(a ?? 0) > 3`. It is `a ?? (0 > 3)` — in a head **and** in a body, because [ADR-066](docs/specification/adr/adr-066.md) D4 put `??` above comparison. That reads oddly and is not the head's business; it is on [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) as a suspicion, with what would have to be true for it to be a defect.
- **Two more findings, neither of them this one's.** A `null` in the **second** hole of an `f"…"` is emitted as `Some(None)` — a double wrap `rustc` refuses — where the first hole, a lone hole, and the same call outside a string are all correct; reproduced identically on the previous build, so it predates this. And the measurement method turned one up on itself: the first baseline for D5 was **wrong**, because the script removing the head's new alternatives matched the first occurrence of the same text in `primary_expr`. It produced a binary that could not parse a tuple anywhere, and it produced numbers — a −92 % that was a parse failure wearing a percentage. The real baseline is `git show 5a1e99a:…/parser/mod.rs`, and §3 of the record says so where the next person will look.

### Added (`&&` and `||` in the head of an `if`, a `while` and a `for`)

- **[ADR-086](docs/specification/adr/adr-086.md).** `if a && b { … }` was a parse error. The head of an `if`, a `while` and a `for` is a grammar of its own, and it exists for one reason — the `{` there opens the **body**, so a brace-led form may not stand in it — but it had been built by copying the ordinary chain and stopping at `head_cmp`, and what stopped there was **not only** the brace-led forms. Part I 3.2 writes a condition with no restriction on what may produce one, and `&&` was in every position but the one a condition is written in.
- **The rule matters more than the two rules** (D3): *a head parses the same language as a body, minus exactly the forms a `{` begins*. A head that differs in any other way is a trap rather than a restriction — the program reads as valid Nikaia and is refused for a reason no rule states, or, worse, is accepted and means something else, which is what the missing `while` rule once did. The chain is now a copy with one difference, in `head_primary`.
- **Why they are safe** (D2): the brace problem is about what stands *immediately before* the `{`. Neither connective can begin a block and neither ends an expression — after each comes another operand, drawn through the same chain from the same brace-free primaries — so the token before the body's brace comes from exactly the set it came from before.
- **`??` is the one level the rule still reaches and is deliberately not taken** (D4). `a ?? b` cannot begin a block either, so the brace reason does not exclude it; what excludes it is that **no program has run into it**, and adding a construct on a principle alone in a record whose other half is evidence mixes two kinds of change. Waiting is free for [ADR-050](docs/specification/adr/adr-050.md) D7's reason: widening what parses breaks nothing, narrowing it does.
- **Cost, measured** (D5): **2,536 instructions per head**, about 1,268 per level, +0.15 % to +0.41 % of a compile on real programs. Same method as [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md) D8 — two builds differing in nothing but these rules, five runs, medians.
- **And it corrected a published number, which is the part worth reading.** [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md) §4's first row says a `while` that stops mid-body costs **−25 %** with a `break`. The baseline it was measured against had to nest an `if` inside its loop *because this gap existed*. The same benchmark, with the shape that is now writable: a flag with `&&` in the head costs **6,500,940** and the `break` costs **6,501,105** — the same, to 165 instructions over two million turns. **The −25 % was the nesting, not the jump.** [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md)'s decision is untouched, because it never rested on that row — what decided it was the `for` row, where a loop cannot be stopped at all and the gap is unbounded in `n`, and [`break-continue-cost.md`](docs/break-continue-cost.md) §7 had already written that leading with the percentages *"would have made a weaker case out of stronger evidence"*. The benchmark keeps the old shape beside the new one rather than replacing it: a benchmark edited under a published number is a number nobody can check.
- **An A/B is about the construct only if the construct is the only difference.** The baseline was carrying two handicaps, this repository had already filed the second as a defect, and nothing connected them — the finding and the mis-attributed number are three paragraphs apart in the same notes page. That is recorded in [`break-continue-cost.md`](docs/break-continue-cost.md) §2.1 rather than quietly fixed.
- **Found by accident, and recorded because it is real**: an `if` *statement*'s head is parsed **twice**. 2,000 `while` heads cost 2,536 instructions each and 2,000 `if` heads cost exactly 5,072, because `assign_stmt` comes before `expr_stmt` and parses the whole `if …` as a candidate assignment target before failing to find a `=`. The ordering is load-bearing, so this is a thing to know rather than a line to change.
- Part I 3.2 gains the connectives and, for the first time, the restriction that *remains* — no brace-led form in a head — because an unstated restriction is what produced this record. the `open-work.md` entry on the connectives closes, the second of the three defects [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md) turned up.

### Fixed (the unconditional loop is lowered to `loop`)

- **[ADR-085](docs/specification/adr/adr-085.md).** [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md) made `while true { … }` a shape somebody would write — until it had a `break`, the only way out was a `return` — and the emitted Rust then said *"denote infinite loops with `loop { … }`"* about a line nobody wrote, with a help naming a keyword this language does not have. [ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md) §3 recorded it as a defect and named the obvious fix, one `#![allow(while_true)]` in the emitted preamble. **That was the wrong remedy and this is the right one**: a `while` whose condition is the literal `true` is emitted as `loop { … }`.
- **It is name for name, not an exception to it** (D2). [ADR-011](docs/specification/adr/adr-011.md) D2 asks *what is the language below's name for this thing*, and [ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md) D1 already settled what the thing is: `while true { … }` **is** this language's unconditional loop. Rust's name for the unconditional loop is `loop`. So `loop` is the translation of the program and `while true` a transcription of its letters — and the letters were what the language below objected to. **The literal only**, never a name that happens to be true: the equivalence is about the written form, so `while running { … }` stays a `while` even where `running` is bound to `true`.
- **Why not the preamble** (D3), stated as a rule rather than as a preference for this case. A suppression there is emitted into every file this compiler will ever write: it does not say *"we looked at this and it is fine"*, it says *"nobody will be shown this again"* — including for the cases nobody has looked at yet, since the next lint would arrive to a precedent. **Where the language below complains about emitted code, the first question is whether it is right.** An `allow` is the answer only after that question has been asked and answered *no*, and then it belongs as near the construct as it can be put.
- **And here it was right, which is why this is not cosmetic** (D4). `while true { }` is `()` below and `loop { }` diverges and is `!` — so `fn a() -> i32 { loop { } }` compiles and the `while` form is an `E0308`. [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) §2.12 wants a function that never returns to stop needing an unreachable `return`, and **no checker change could have delivered that while the lowering emitted the form `rustc` refuses**: the checker would have stopped refusing and `rustc` would have refused instead. That entry loses its blocker and keeps only its checker half.
- **Measured rather than assumed**: every `.nika` in `examples/` and `tests/samples/` lowered and compiled, and `while_true` appears nowhere in the corpus. Three tests — the lowering, the name-that-is-not-the-literal, and that the loop still means what it meant, which is run rather than read.
- **The same survey filed one defect and deliberately left two alone.** A `catch { … }` whose handler never mentions the error emits `Err(error) => …`, so `rustc` says *"unused variable: `error`"* about a name **the emitter wrote** — the author wrote `catch { 1000 }`. That is the same class and it is on [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) with its reproduction and the over-approximate walk that would close it. The other two, `unused variable: x` and `k`, name bindings the **user** wrote: actionable message, wrong file, a smaller and different problem.

### Added (`break` and `continue`)

- **[ADR-084](docs/specification/adr/adr-084.md).** [ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md) D2 found, while answering a different question, that a Nikaia loop could only be left by its condition going false or by a `return` that leaves the whole function. It named the two missing words and did not decide them; [ADR-071](docs/specification/adr/adr-071.md) reserved them so the question could stay open for free. **This answers it: `break` leaves the innermost loop, `continue` starts its next turn**, and the reservation is what makes the addition cost no program a name.
- **Built and measured before it was decided**, which is not the usual order and was the right one here: a keyword is the most expensive thing a language adds ([ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md) D1), so *"what does it cost"* deserved numbers rather than an estimate. [`docs/break-continue-cost.md`](docs/break-continue-cost.md) is the measurement in full and the record's evidence; everything the record decides about boundaries, about a `catch` handler, about rule placement was **found by building it** and would not have been in a design document written first.
- **What decided it is not a percentage.** −25 % on a `while` that learns it is finished mid-body and −1 % on a `continue` are numbers a language can live without. The `for` row is different in kind: **a `for` could not be stopped at all** — `return` leaves the function and a flag stops only the body — so at n = 2 000 000 the shape that replaced a `break` took two million turns to do 1 415 turns' work, and the gap is unbounded in `n` rather than a constant factor. A loop that cannot stop is not a slow loop; it is a loop that has to be written as something else.
- **`break x` is refused** (`NK1133`, D3). Rust's `break` carries a value out of a `loop` and this one carries none, so a value after it parses as a second statement — the program compiles, the value is dropped, and nothing says so. That is the *three statements, no error* the `while` rule's comment records from the day `while` was missing, and it is worse here because the author wrote something that means one thing in the language beside this one.
- **A jump does not leave a function, and the four places that means something are refused by name** (`NK1132`, D4): a lambda is a Rust closure, a task an `async move` block ([ADR-055](docs/specification/adr/adr-055.md) §6), an `overlap` branch one `async` block per branch ([ADR-050](docs/specification/adr/adr-050.md) D2), and a DSL fold's `init`/`step`/`merge` closures handed to the driver. The message names which of the four stands in the way; without it the answer is `rustc`'s *"`break` outside of a loop"* about a file nobody wrote (Part III C.1). The restriction worth knowing before writing code is the lock doors ([ADR-065](docs/specification/adr/adr-065.md)), whose block is a lambda — and there it is doing work, because the alternative is a lock held across a jump.
- **A `catch` handler is not one of them** (D5), because it lowers to a `match` arm. D4's table makes the opposite look like the rule, so this is a decision with a test that **runs** rather than an omission from a table.
- **The refusal is in the lowering as well, and that one is the guarantee** (D6). The checker's boundaries are found by a walk and a walk missed a corner: the block walk that reaches a fold's lambdas does not descend into a `spawn`, because a task's body is a detached context everywhere else it is asked about. The fix is not a better walk — `Flow` gained `in_loop`, true in a loop's body and false in `Flow::PLAIN`, where a lambda, a task, an `overlap` branch and a function body each start. Every statement is emitted through one place. [ADR-055](docs/specification/adr/adr-055.md) §6's division applied again: **the checker is the diagnostic and the lowering is the guarantee**, which is the general answer to *"is this walk complete?"* — a question no walk can answer about itself.
- **No labels** (D2), and §3 is the argument rather than the taste: six analyses gained an arm for the two statements and **every one of those arms does nothing**, because all six are about where a value goes and a jump carries none. A label is a name that is not a value, scoped to a construct rather than a block, and it would give each of the six a question it does not have. The condition for reopening it is a **measurement** of the shape it does not reach, not an opinion.
- **The grammar rules stand last in the statement alternation, and that is a decision with a number on it** (D8). Placed where they read most naturally, ahead of `assign_stmt`, they are tried and fail for every statement that is not a jump: ~740 instructions a statement, +1.28 % on a statement-dense file. Placed last, what reaches them is only the `}` that ends a block, where the whole alternation fails anyway: **665 instructions per block**, +0.13 % to +0.32 % of a compile. It costs nothing in correctness, because both words are reserved and no earlier arm can swallow a jump. The comment at the rules carries the reason, because an ordering with no reason attached is one the next person tidies.
- **[ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md) D1 stands and `loop` stays reserved and unused.** Its condition for reopening is a `break` that hands back a **value** — *`while true` whose `break` hands back a value reads as a lie* — and D3 refuses one. What does change is that `while true { … }` becomes usable: until now it could only be left by `return`, which is why no `.nika` file in the tree writes one.
- **The ordering analysis learns a fourth way to divert.** [ADR-034](docs/specification/adr/adr-034.md) refuses to overlap a statement whose `catch` handler can `return`, because what follows is then conditional on it having succeeded. A handler that *jumps* makes it conditional the same way, so `diverts` counts `break` and `continue` — and is exact about the one case where it must not: a jump bound to a loop written **inside** the handler lands in the handler. A rule written ahead of the case that needs it, because an analysis should be right about what a construct means rather than about which constructs happen to reach it.
- **Three pre-existing defects found on the way**, all in [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md), one of them half-closed: `while true` puts a `rustc` warning on a generated line (not caused by this, **made common** by it, and its one-line fix is a decision about every file this compiler writes); a grammar fold's three lambdas are **not checked at all**, so an undeclared name in one reaches the backend — D4 closes only the half a jump can reach; and `&&` and `||` are missing from the head of an `if`, a `while` and a `for`, found by writing `while i < n && running`, the very workaround a language without `break` needs.
- **[`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) §2.12 is rewritten rather than edited away.** Its argument for why removing the unreachable `return` after a `while true` is cheap *here* was *"because `break` and `continue` do not exist"*. The test is now *"the condition is the literal `true` **and** no `break` in the body is bound to this loop"* — still small, because the checker already keeps the loop count that answers it, and no longer the sentence that was written.
- **Everything else**: Part I 3.3 documents both with examples that were run; 2.1's note about the reserved non-constructs now names only `loop`; `NK1132` and `NK1133` join Part III C.3's table, whose Status range had also lost `NK1125` since it was written; `tests/samples/jumps.nika` is the shape Part I documents, walked by the parse, type-check and editor-grammar corpora; and the VS Code grammar scopes both as `keyword.control.flow` beside `return` — `keyword.control.loop` is the word that opens a loop and these two leave one.

### Fixed (a field of a borrowed subject may not be handed out by value)

- **[ADR-083](docs/specification/adr/adr-083.md), `NK1131`.** `return self.username` out of a `&self` method was `error[E0507]: cannot move out of `self.username` which is behind a shared reference` — about a file nobody wrote, and that is **every accessor** over a field that is not a number, since `&self` is what Part I 4.2 writes for a method that only reads.
- **Part I 6.8 decides this and had already**, which is why it is a refusal rather than a judgement call: *"Ownership rules occasionally reject code […] every such error explains itself in plain language and tells you what to do next. You never need Rust knowledge to read a Nikaia error; if a raw internal (Rust) error ever reaches you, that is a Nikaia bug."* Both halves were being broken at once — the rule rejected, and it rejected in the wrong compiler's words.
- **Not a `.clone()` the emitter writes.** That was the other option and it is against a decision already made: [ADR-064](docs/specification/adr/adr-064.md) D2 wrote *a hull you can see is one you write*, and a copy the author cannot see is the same thing one position over — an allocation in the hot path that nothing in the source accounts for, in a chapter whose whole argument is that a value's cost is visible. It would also buy nothing: both ways out are one word and the message names them.
- **Measured as a class before anything was built.** A field of a borrowed subject leaks in exactly three positions — handed back, bound, passed — and three shapes work: a field that copies, a written `.clone()`, and a `self` receiver. The rule reads the **field's type** rather than counting `&self`s, and the positions are the three that were measured: a position missed leaves today's behaviour, and a position wrongly included breaks a program that works (C.4).
- **A free call needed its own call site**, because it walks its arguments itself rather than going through `arguments_given` — found by the class measurement coming back two-of-three instead of three-of-three.
- **A message that names a way out has to be a way out, and this one was not.** `.clone()` had no ledger entry, so `sync::infer` could not vouch for the call and the method around it came out as one that **pauses** — at which point [ADR-080](docs/specification/adr/adr-080.md)'s `NK1129` refused it for disagreeing with its trait. Taking `NK1131`'s advice made a different diagnostic fire. Found by the trait tests' own fixture, which was also the first program the new refusal caught writing `return self.name` out of a `&self` method — a program that had never compiled. `String::clone` and `str::clone` are in `std.contracts` now.
- **D4: the message leads with the way out that costs nothing**, and names the type the field actually has — `&str` for text, `&Vec[i64]` for a list. The other two each take something: an allocation, or the whole subject.
- **That third way out was first filed as missing, and it was not.** `fn name_of(&self) -> &str { return &self.name }` compiles and runs — the `&` is [Part I 6.5](docs/specification/10-nikaia-light.md)'s own spelling (`let name = &config.name`), and `NK1104`'s help had been saying *"write `&` to take a view of it"* on the same screen. What is refused is the same line **without** the `&`, which is a `String` in a `&str` slot: `NK1102` at a parameter and `NK1103` at a `let`, so the rule is uniform and the accessor is not special. The `E0106` the filing also claimed came from `rustc` reading a **stale** file, since a refused program writes none. Both were a minute's work to check.

### Changed (a grammar is entered by a call)

- **[ADR-082](docs/specification/adr/adr-082.md)** withdraws `dsl <grammar> from <expr>` for an ordinary call — `Json.value(input)`. The **block** form, `dsl <driver> { … } eod`, is untouched: its body must not be read as Nikaia at all, which no call can arrange, and that is what earns it a keyword.
- **`dsl` spelled two constructs with nothing in common, and took its name from two different places.** `dsl Json from x` looks `Json` up among the file's **grammars** and refuses if there is none; `dsl mysql { … } eod` does not resolve its target to a grammar at all — Part II 10.2's own status note says so — because `mysql` names a **driver**. One keyword, two namespaces. After this, `dsl` takes a driver name and only a driver name.
- **And the same line meant two things by position.** At run time `dsl Json from "config.json"` parses the eleven characters `config.json`; in a build-time binding it was meant to parse that file's contents. The specification presented that as a feature — *"the same syntax … follows from the context, not from a different spelling"* — and it is the one thing this project refuses everywhere else. Now three visible steps say it: `comptime` **when**, `from "…"` **where the bytes come from**, the call **what is done with them**.
- **D2: every `pub` rule is an entry, and the distinguished one was ours.** `winnow-grammar` generates one function per public rule named after it (`Cron::parse_schedule()`) and has no main rule — the pick is this compiler's, and it is `find(|r| r.is_public && par_fold_of(r).is_some()).or_else(…)`: the **first** public rule wins, and a `par_fold` one beats an earlier one, by source order, silently. The call form removes the question rather than fixing the pick, and with it the refusal *"grammar `…` has no `pub` rule to enter through"*.
- **D3: no `parse` alias.** One that exists only while a grammar has exactly one public rule is a name that appears and disappears with its neighbours; one a grammar must declare says a second time what the rule name already says. A program that wants `Json.parse(x)` names its rule `parse`.
- Nothing is built — a grammar name is not yet accepted as a callee — and the old form is to be **taken back rather than deprecated**, because no program in this tree writes it and that is the free moment.

### Fixed (a `+` over text is a call, and an expression gets a span to key it by)

- **[ADR-081](docs/specification/adr/adr-081.md).** Three of the four ways a program can join text went to the language below and were refused there — Rust's `String + &str` rule reaching the user unchanged, about a file nobody wrote. And the checker got it wrong **first**, which is the worse half: `"a" + s` came to a `&str`, so a function declaring `-> String` was refused as `NK1104` — a **false** refusal, and Part III C.4 says this compiler never refuses a correct program. Part I 4.7 wrote that line as its own example.
- **D1: `Expr::Binary` carries a span, the first expression-level one in this AST.** `contracts/sync.rs` has called them open work in its own words since it was written. It exists because the answer had to be keyed by **which** `+` rather than by the statement around it: `a + b + c` is two of them, and every other answer in [ADR-028](docs/specification/adr/adr-028.md)'s channel is keyed by a statement because every other answer is about one thing per statement. One variant and not every expression, because a field nobody reads is a field that goes stale.
- **D2: the shapes become `nikaia_std::concat::plus`, and the result is a `String`.** A trait with an impl per shape, which is [ADR-048](docs/specification/adr/adr-048.md) D1's own sentence a third time — and it costs nothing: three million concatenations take 42 ms as `String + &str` lowered before, 250 ms through a `format!` for every shape, and **42 ms** through the trait. The single obvious rule would have been a sixfold regression on the one form that already worked; the rule turns out to *be* the table of four cases, written once in `std` instead of four times in the emitter. The `String` is a decision rather than an inference: a concatenation makes a new value, and saying `&str` is what made `-> String` a false refusal.
- **D3: a number's `+` does not move, and that is what keeps D2 narrow.** Arithmetic inside `nikaia_std` would silently lose [ADR-043](docs/specification/adr/adr-043.md) D1's overflow abort — `overflow-checks` is on per Nikaia crate and off for the profile, because a foreign crate's hash function wraps on purpose. Measured: `a + b` in the checked crate aborts, and the same `a + b` through an `#[inline]` helper in the unchecked one wraps silently to `-9223372036854775808`. **Inlining does not carry the check across**; it is decided where the code is written. Written down as a trap for the whole `index::at` family rather than left to be met again — that one escapes it only because its conversion is an explicit `try_from`.
- **Part I 6's `println(prefix + "System started")` compiles**, and the specification sweep's compile baseline moved one line from `error[E0369]` to `compiles`.
- **Taking the concatenation out of Part I 4.7's line uncovered a second defect underneath it**, filed as `open-work.md` §1.1: a `&self` method cannot hand back a field it owns (`E0507`), which is every accessor over a `String` or a `Vec` and has nothing to do with text. So the page keeps its interpolated form, and closing that entry is written down as the condition for putting its own sentence back.

### Changed (the string-concatenation entry said two things that measurement contradicts)

- **The allocation it conceded does not exist.** `docs/open-work.md` §1.1 offered `format!` for every shape as *one rule rather than a table of four cases*, at the cost of an allocation where `String + &str` reuses today. A trait in `std` with an impl per shape — the arrangement [ADR-048](docs/specification/adr/adr-048.md) D1 already uses twice — makes the rule **be** the table: three million concatenations take 42 ms as `String + &str` lowers today, 250 ms through `format!`, and **42 ms through the trait**. So `format!` everywhere would be a 6× regression on the one form that works, and the trait is free.
- **And what actually rules the trait out is arithmetic, which is a trap worth naming.** A trait over `+` would take numbers too, and a number's `+` may not leave this language's own crates: [ADR-043](docs/specification/adr/adr-043.md) D1 turns `overflow-checks` on **per Nikaia crate** while the profile turns them off, because a foreign crate's hash function wraps on purpose — and `nikaia_std` is foreign to that split. Measured with the same profile shape: `a + b` in the checked crate **aborts**, and the same `a + b` through an `#[inline]` helper in the unchecked crate **wraps silently** to `-9223372036854775808`. Inlining does not carry the check across; it is decided where the code is written.
- **So the fix is the option the entry had dismissed:** a trait for the string shapes, and the checker telling the emitter which `+` is one. That needs a key, and `Expr::Binary` has no span to be keyed by — which `contracts/sync.rs` already names as open work in its own words.
- **`index::at` and `index::set` are unaffected** and it is worth saying why: the first converts with an explicit `try_from` and the second does no arithmetic at all. The trap is specific to moving an operator into `std`.

### Changed (the whole-workspace flake loses its hypothesis and gains a shape)

- **The stdin explanation is refuted.** `docs/open-work.md` §3.1 held that concurrent probes shared the wrapper's inherited stdin; running the whole test with `< /dev/null` fails identically three runs out of three, so the caller's stdin decides nothing.
- **What the failures look like is sharper.** The probe's `--print` output now arrives complete, and what fails is compiling the source on stdin — which turns out to be a `rustc` *diagnostic* about a malformed `#[diagnostic::on_unimplemented]` attribute. No such attribute exists anywhere in this repository and the string is `rustc`'s own, so it is not something this compiler wrote or relayed.
- **What lands on stdin is a whole rendered diagnostic**, not a fragment: the offending line is line **7** of the input, and lines 1 to 6 are the `error:`, `-->` and caret lines above it. So it is another process's stderr crossing into a probe's stdin, rather than one stream being cut short — which is what the first two sightings looked like. The producing crate is not identified; `serde` carries `#[diagnostic::on_unimplemented]` in the graph but in the form this toolchain accepts.
- **And it clusters in time rather than in the code.** On one commit, seven consecutive whole-suite runs passed — two immediately after a full rebuild — and then eight failed. In the failing window a clean `origin/main` built from scratch fails identically; in the passing window it passes. Ruled out so far, in order: the tests, the wrapper's own code path, a stale binary, a rebuild immediately before, and the caller's stdin.

### Fixed (an `impl` owes its `trait`, and a write through the brackets is not an index)

- **[ADR-080](docs/specification/adr/adr-080.md).** Two defects found the same way — by running the programs the specification prints — and closed together because each had a shape the language below already gave.
- **`NK1130`: an `impl` and the `trait` it names disagreeing about which methods exist.** [ADR-078](docs/specification/adr/adr-078.md) made the declaration possible and said in its own §4 that nothing checked against it, so a method the trait does not declare, or one it declares that the `impl` leaves out, went to `rustc` as `E0407` and `E0046`. Two messages under one code, because a reader is doing two different things: moving a method, and finishing an `impl`.
- **`NK1129`: an implementation that pauses where the declaration cannot say so.** [ADR-078](docs/specification/adr/adr-078.md) D4 asserts `sync` for a trait's methods and had to — `No` would have made every call through a bound an `.await`. An implementation whose body pauses therefore lowered to `async fn` against a plain `fn`, and the language below answered *"method `load` has an incompatible type for trait"*. Refused here, because `async fn` in a trait is a thing this emitter has no way to ask for; the way to allow it is a word in the declaration that says a method may pause, and this language has only `sync` for the opposite.
- **A trait this unit does not declare is not checked against**, which is the rule rather than a gap: `impl Error for ConfigError` names the one trait the compiler reads rather than one a `.nika` file wrote ([ADR-023](docs/specification/adr/adr-023.md) D3), and silence is the only correct answer about a declaration that is not here.
- **A walk of its own, and the ledger's `sync` column is why.** The type checker runs *before* `sync::infer` — that order is what keeps [ADR-027](docs/specification/adr/adr-027.md) sound, since the checker resolves the calls the inference reads — so a rule needing the finished column runs from `check_program` beside `dsl::check` and `views::check`.
- **D2: Part I 4.5's own three-line map example now compiles.** `scores["Player1"] = 100` failed with `error[E0282]: type annotations needed for HashMap<_, i32, BuildHasherDefault<FxHasher>>`, in a message naming `TrustedMap`, `BuildHasherDefault<FxHasher>` and a type parameter `K` — three spellings the program never wrote, which is Part III C.1 at its worst because it names this compiler's own internal word for a map.
- **Why the key stayed unknown is not what it looks like.** Rust's `Index` for a map is over whatever the key **borrows** as, so indexing with a `&str` is satisfied by `String`, `&str` and `Box<str>` alike and pins nothing. `insert` takes the key by value and pins it exactly — so a **write** through the brackets lowers to `nikaia_std::index::set`, which is `insert` for a map and the indexed assignment it already was for a sequence. A trait and not two emitter cases, which is [ADR-048](docs/specification/adr/adr-048.md) D1's own sentence a second time: this emitter does not know types, and `xs[0] = 5` and `scores[k] = v` are the same three tokens.
- **Only the plain `=`.** A compound `m[k] += 1` reads the slot as well as writing it, so it is an `Index` either way — and what *reading* an absent key means is a question of its own.
- **The specification sweep's compile baseline moved one line**, from `error[E0282]` to `compiles`, which is what that baseline is for. Its report is also addressed by a block's **ordinal** now rather than by its line: a line number moves whenever a paragraph above the block gains a sentence, and one such edit churned twenty-one lines without changing a single verdict.

### Changed (what crosses from build time to run time)

- **[ADR-079](docs/specification/adr/adr-079.md)** decides the bridge. [ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md) built the binding and [ADR-075](docs/specification/adr/adr-075.md) said what its initialiser may *do*; neither said what may **cross**. It is not free: the destination is a constant in the generated file, and a constant cannot own memory — so a parse that produces a `Vec[Row]` parses perfectly well and has nowhere to land.
- **D1: a result crosses in its view form** — `Vec[T]` as `&[T]`, `String` as `&str`. Not a new distinction: it is the one Part I already makes between a buffer and a view, at the place where it is forced rather than chosen. It also coincides with where the language below draws its own line (`const X: &[T]` exists, `const X: Vec<T>` cannot), so the two boundaries need no negotiation. **D2** refuses what owns memory **by its type** rather than by its parse, and rejects the alternative of building it once at startup — that gives up what the feature is for.
- **D3 is the one that answers the motivating case, and it answers it by not needing anything new.** A map that crosses is **fixed** — and that is not tidiness: **a closed key set is the precondition for a perfect hash, and the crossing is where it closes.** The property arrives by itself. So `ROUTES.get(name)` is what a program writes, and whether that is a perfect hash, a binary search or a `match` is the compiler's, exactly as [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) already picks a hasher from key provenance with nothing in the source mentioning hashing.
- **D4: no `PerfectHashMap`, no `build()` to call.** The measurement is the argument: over 200 keys a `match` beats a hash map **9.0 ns against 18.0** — and that is a statement about 200 keys, not about a million. A decision whose right answer moves with the size of the input belongs where the size is known, and that is not the source. A program may say *this map is fixed*; it may not say how the compiler should honour that.
- **D5: a serialised blob is a size optimisation for later and admits no new types**, recorded so it is not re-proposed. The bytes are baked on one machine and read on another, and this compiler has two targets whose pointer widths differ — so only fixed-width, pointer-free fields survive, which is D1 again from the other side. The language below promises no layout without a `repr` the program never asked for, and padding bytes are a hazard to [ADR-005](docs/specification/adr/adr-005.md) D8's CI-enforced byte-determinism. And **emitting a literal already is serialisation**, done by the linker, which knows the target and needs no unsafe cast.
- **Nothing is built**, and §3 names what it would take: a fixed map type in `std`, an evaluator that can loop and `push` (which is the real work), and — for the case where somebody writes the pairs by hand rather than parsing them — the **list literal**, which Part I 4.5 already records as unbuilt. Which has the odd consequence that the toy version of this feature is harder to reach than the real one.

### Changed (the whole-workspace test failure gets a mechanism)

- **`docs/open-work.md` §3.1 stops being a suspicion about nothing.** The failure still does not reproduce on demand — the same command minutes later passes on this branch and on a clean `origin/main`, three runs out of three each — but a third sighting showed `rustc` being handed, as **source** at `<anon>:7:55`, a line that is a `rustc` *diagnostic*. `<anon>` is standard input, which is where cargo puts the probe's source, so something other than that source was on the wrapper's stdin.
- **What it points at:** the wrapper passes an invocation it does not lower straight through, so the child inherits stdin; under `cargo test --workspace` many cargo instances probe at once and share one. Two readers on one pipe would take each other's bytes, which is the shape of both symptoms seen — a truncated read and a read of somebody else's output. Recorded as a hypothesis with evidence, because the next step is to measure it rather than to change the wrapper on a story.

### Added (every program in the specification is compiled, by a test)

- **A page can be wrong in a way nothing notices.** `docs/README.md` §1 already makes a stale **Status** note a defect in its own right; `crates/nikaia/tests/specification.rs` applies the same rule to the code beside it. It takes every `nika` block in the three pages as far as it goes — parse, check, lower — and hands the ones that lower to `rustc`, against two recorded baselines.
- **Of 122 blocks, 52 are programs this compiler takes and 28 of those compile below.** The other 24 name a `User`, a `Config`, a `postgres` or an `http` the chapter around them declares, which is a fragment rather than a defect: `NK1117` deliberately does not refuse a name this compiler cannot see (Part III C.4). The baselines are what tell the two apart without anybody re-deciding it each time, and a block that stops lowering — or starts — is a line in a diff.
- **A block is not always a program, and that shaped the walk.** Four readings are tried and the furthest wins: as written, items plus a `main`, a body wrapped in one, and items followed by statements — the last being the shape a chapter uses most and the one neither obvious reading can take. A block with an elision where code would be is marked a **sketch** and not counted against anything.
- **Two findings were on the page**, and both are fixed. Part I 4.7's `return "User: " + self.username` was refused twice over — the checker said it hands back a `&str` where `String` is declared, and the lowering would not have compiled either — and three plain strings in Part I 7 held `{p}`, `{line}` and `{expected}`, written before [ADR-035](docs/specification/adr/adr-035.md) D5 made only `f"…"` interpolate. A test now asserts that Part I 2.5 is the only block allowed to trip `NK1111`, because that section is *about* the difference.
- **Two are in the compiler and are filed as choices rather than repairs.** Three of the four string concatenations go to the language below and are refused there — only `String + &str` compiles, so Rust's own rule reaches the user unchanged — and the checker types `"a" + s` as a `&str` first, which makes a function declaring `-> String` a **false** refusal. And Part I 4.5's three-line map example fails with `E0282` in a message naming `TrustedMap`, `BuildHasherDefault<FxHasher>` and a type parameter `K`: three spellings the program never wrote, which is Part III C.1 at its worst. `docs/open-work.md` §1.2 and §1.3 have the measurements and what each would cost to close.
- **One assertion of the test's own was wrong and is worth the sentence.** It said a block the page presents as a refusal ought to be refused, and named six stale ones. All six were fine: every page in this specification shows the offending line **commented out**, so the block itself compiles and should. The marker is now called `about-a-refusal`, which is what it detects, and the third test counts how much of the specification is a program instead — a floor to raise, not a property to assert.

### Added (a trait can be declared, and a type parameter can be bound by one)

- **[ADR-078](docs/specification/adr/adr-078.md).** Part I 4.7 writes `trait Summarize { … }` as its own example and it was a parse error at the keyword: a trait could be **implemented** and not **declared**, so the traits a program could implement were the ones `std` and the Rust side already name. That lived in a Status note and nowhere else — and a Status note is not `open-work.md`, so the item the specification's own example needs had never been ranked against anything. [ADR-074](docs/specification/adr/adr-074.md) §4 made it load-bearing: a bound names a trait, so `[T: Summarize]` had nothing to name.
- **D1: a trait declares signatures, and the shape says so.** `ast::TraitMethod` has no body — not an `Item::Fn` with an empty block and not one whose body became an `Option`, because the difference between *"declares this method"* and *"this method does nothing"* is the whole of what a trait is, and a body that is absent cannot be accidentally emitted. The emitter gets a second, smaller writer for the same reason: `function` reads the ledger for `sync`, for each position's `Shared` count and for whether the call can fail, and every one of those is an answer about a *body*.
- **D3 is the reuse that made this small: a bound and a receiver ask one question.** A trait's methods are recorded as `Summarize::summary` — the same key shape an `impl`'s methods get — so a member reached on a bounded parameter is looked up by asking the call again on the trait. The bound therefore answers the **whole** call: arity is `NK1101` from the declaration, argument types `NK1102`, the result type is what a `let` or `return` is checked against, and `sync`, `throws` and the signature's type variables come from the same entry. `NK1126` is unchanged where no bound declares the member, which is what keeps it honest — a bound is not a blanket licence.
- **D2: `[T: Summarize]`, and `[T: A + B]` for several**, written where Part I 4.6 already puts the parameter.
- **D4: a trait's method is `sync` in the ledger, and that is a decision.** `sync::infer` raises a function from `No` to `Inferred` by reading its body; a declaration has none, so `No` would stand — and `No` means *pauses*. Measured: `fn shout[T: Summarize]` came out as `async fn shout` awaiting a `String`. Asserting it is the only answer this position can write, because `async fn` in a trait is something the emitter has no way to ask for. Trait methods also join `sync::infer`'s graph as unblocked leaves, since a callee **absent** from that fixpoint was read as one that pauses.
- **`trait` is a reserved word now, which it was not** — [ADR-076](docs/specification/adr/adr-076.md)'s sweep found it as a *name*, because `let trait = 3` was a legal program.
- **Part I 4.7's program runs and prints `User: Ada`**, which with [ADR-074](docs/specification/adr/adr-074.md) finishes chapter 4's generics section end to end.
- **Three things went wrong in the order they had to, and the third was not about traits.** The bound parsed and emitted and the checker still refused, because a program's ledger is **absorbed** from its unit's and `traits` was the one map `absorb_renaming` left behind — visible only as a third resolution pass with an empty map where the first two had it. Then the lowering awaited a `String`, which is D4. Then the fixture itself did not compile: Part I 4.7's `"User: " + self.username` is `&str + String`, accepted here and refused below. Both that and a trait whose method genuinely pauses are in `open-work.md` with their reproductions, named as choices rather than repairs.
- **What is not built:** nothing checks an `impl` against the trait it names, so a method with a different signature — or a missing one — is refused by `rustc` rather than here. That is the next piece and it is a refusal, which costs nothing today. A default body has no syntax, a trait is not a type, a bound cannot stand on an `impl`'s own parameter, and a trait cannot be named from another package.

### Changed (the word is `comptime`)

- **[ADR-077](docs/specification/adr/adr-077.md) amends [ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md) D1's spelling and nothing else.** `const` was taken without an argument because it is the word every neighbouring language uses, and it is wrong **here** for a reason only this language has: Part I 2.1 already makes every binding immutable unless it says `mut`, so a keyword meaning *this one does not change* distinguishes nothing. What the declaration promises is a **time** — evaluated while the program is built, refused if it cannot be — and `comptime` is what says that.
- **Zig is the precedent and the mirror.** The language that made compile-time evaluation a first-class stage did not call it `const` either — and calls *its* immutable binding `const`, which is the opposite assignment, for a language whose default is the other way round.
- **`const` stays reserved rather than released** (D2), on [ADR-050](docs/specification/adr/adr-050.md) D7's polarity: un-reserving is the free direction and reserving is not. It is the first thing a reader arriving from another language will type, and a reserved word can be given a message where a name cannot — `` `const` is a reserved word, so it is not a name ``. The list goes from 33 to 34: one keyword **more**, not one instead.
- **The word does not travel downwards** (D3). What the emitter writes is Rust's `const`, because that is Rust's name for the same slot: `comptime LIMIT = 4 * 1024` arrives as `const LIMIT: i32 = 4096;`. The test that covers the lowering now says which word is on which side, so it does not read as an inconsistency.
- Everything else [ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md) decided stands: where it may stand, the optional type, that the fold *must* succeed, what may be in the initialiser, and that it is a value rather than a place. `NK1127` keeps its number and changes one word.
- Caught on the way: the reserved-word array had been re-wrapped by `cargo fmt` since it was last edited, so a replacement matched nothing and passed silently. The specification's table and `RESERVED_WORDS` are compared mechanically again — 34 against 34.

### Fixed (a name the language below reserves no longer reaches it unescaped)

- **[ADR-076](docs/specification/adr/adr-076.md).** `let type = 3` lowered to `let type = 3;` and `rustc` answered *"expected identifier, found keyword `type`"* about a file nobody wrote, with *"escape `type` to use it as an identifier"* as the help — advice that means nothing in this language. [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)'s class, and this compiler had it in **every** position a name can stand in: a local, a parameter, a field, a function's own name, a struct, an enum, a variant, a lambda's argument, a `for` binding.
- **D1: escaped where it is written, not reserved here.** `type` becomes `r#type` in the generated file and stays `type` in the source, in every diagnostic and in the ledger. Reserving the twenty-seven words instead would let the backend decide what this language's vocabulary is — `type` is the field name of every tagged record anybody has written, and `move`, `ref`, `static` and `final` are ordinary words. [ADR-051](docs/specification/adr/adr-051.md) D1's *reserving costs nothing before programs exist* is about words **this** language needs for its own constructs, and these are not them. The cause is [ADR-011](docs/specification/adr/adr-011.md) D2 writing a name for a name, so the fix belongs where the name is written — [ADR-056](docs/specification/adr/adr-056.md)'s *a rule and not a list* at the same boundary one position over.
- **D2: at the emitter's name position and never at `text`.** One line there would have covered everything at once and been a silent defect: `text` is also what the checker's recorded answers are keyed by — `pausing_methods`, `fallible_methods`, `nullable_fields` all keyed by the name the source wrote — so escaping there would make every one of those lookups miss, on exactly the programs this is for.
- **D3, `NK1128`: three names are refused, because there is no escape to write.** Rust's own reply to `r#crate` is *"`crate` cannot be a raw identifier"*, and the same for `super`, `self` and `Self`. So the one rule has one forced exception and it is the **target's** rather than a choice made here; `self` keeps `NK1119`, which says more. Three words rather than twenty-seven is the point of D1: `crate` and `super` name a module tree this language does not have, and `Self` is already how it writes the type an `impl` is on.
- **D4 puts both questions about a declared name in one call**, which is how the gap that was waiting got closed: `NK1119`'s walk never covered an item's **own** name, because `struct self` cannot parse — and `struct crate` parses fine and went straight through.
- **The measurement was wrong twice and the method is what fixed it.** The first sweep lowered `let W = 3` and read `rustc`'s *"found keyword"*: twenty-four words. It missed `crate`, `super` and `box`, which fail there with `E0532`, `E0433` and *"expected pattern"* instead. **Twenty-seven**, found by compiling every word in every position and asking only whether the program builds. That sweep is now the test, and it is what proves coverage rather than arguing it — an escape applied at the position that *declares* a name and missed where one is *referred to* is worse than no escape at all. Two of its own first versions failed for reasons that were the test's (a local shadowing a function of the same name; a `let` that needed `let mut`), and both are written into it.
- **`struct Event { type: String }` compiles, runs and prints what was put in it**, which is the program this was worth doing for.

### Added (`const` is a declaration, inside a body)

- **[ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md) D2's body form is built**: `const LIMIT = 4 * 1024` parses, is evaluated while the program is built, and reaches the language below as `const LIMIT: i32 = 4096;` — **the arithmetic does not survive**, which is the visible half of D3. Six tests cover it, one of which compiles and runs the program rather than reading the emitted file.
- **What the keyword adds is a demand, not arithmetic.** The compiler folded constants long before this ([ADR-063](docs/specification/adr/adr-063.md)), so the new part is that the fold *has* to succeed: **a `let` may fold; a `const` must, and says so where it cannot** — `NK1127`, whose help names the way out, `let`, for a value that was never a constant. Refused rather than computed later, because a guarantee that silently degrades into *it happened to be cheap* is worse than one that is absent.
- **The checker hands the emitter both the type and the value**, and the second half is the one that is easy to miss: Rust's `const` takes a type and this emitter has none ([ADR-028](docs/specification/adr/adr-028.md)) — but it also has no **scope**, and folding `const PAIR = PAGE * 2` means knowing what `PAGE` is. Without the value travelling with the type, a constant built out of another would reach Rust unfolded and D3's demand would be `rustc`'s to keep. A test covers exactly that shape.
- What the initialiser may hold is D5's first stage: an integer — a literal, arithmetic over literals and over other constants — and `true` or `false`. A call is the second stage, so `dsl … from "…"` still has nothing to stand in.
- **Item level is not built** and is on [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) §2.14 with what it needs: a scope frame under every function, which the checker does not have because its scope is a stack pushed per function. No decision is missing — the evaluator, the refusal and the type spelling are the body form's.
- The error corpus moved by **two lines**: `return` now appears in the *also possible here* list of two parse errors, because the statement rule gained an alternative. Strictly more informative, and regenerated rather than hand-edited.

### Fixed (two records were wearing one number, and the checker now says so)

- **ADR-074 was claimed twice on the same afternoon and the second record overwrote the first.** The generics record — *a type parameter is a type inside its own body and a variable at every call site* — landed at 17:53; the build-time-body record was written over the same file at 19:14. The generics record is **restored from its own commit, unchanged**, and keeps 074, because it was there first. The build-time-body record is now [ADR-075](docs/specification/adr/adr-075.md), and every citation of it was moved with it. Part I 2.1's sentence about `const` had also drifted to 074 and points at [ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md) D1 again, which is where the word is reserved.
- **The cause was a claim read in the wrong place.** The reserved table said nothing was claimed — correctly, because a claim is deleted in the commit that adds the record — and the record was then written to a path that already held one. `cat >` does not ask.
- **`scripts/check-adr-refs.py` now checks that every number belongs to exactly one record**, which is the part a machine can keep: one index row per number, one file per number, and a file whose own title agrees with its name. Every citation still resolved while this was broken — the file was there and still had a `D1` — but the index carried **two rows for 074**, and that is what is now read. Confirmed by reintroducing the collision: the check fires and names the number. CI runs this script, so the next one goes red in minutes rather than surviving three commits.
- It also fired once on ADR-030 before being narrowed: that file's second row is in the **amendments** table, whose second cell is another ADR rather than prose. Two tables in one file open the same way, and telling them apart is what the check does now.

### Fixed (the overlap report answered the question wrongly)

- **`--overlaps` printed *"which meet on nothing"* as a fixed header over every block**, without consulting a single verdict — so a block the checker refuses on the very next line was described as meeting on nothing. That is the one answer this tool must never give: it exists because *"the refusals are the compiler's own … that is only fair if the refusals can be asked about"*, and a reader consults it precisely when the checker has said no. It now runs the same pairwise verdicts `check` runs and names the first refused pair and why — `branches 1 and 2 may not run together - both reach stdout, and one writes it`. The test covers both directions, so a fix that hard-codes the other verdict fails it too.

### Added (the shipped ledger answers what it touches)

- **Twenty-four `sync` entries in `std.contracts` had no touch answer** ([ADR-075](docs/specification/adr/adr-075.md) D2 needs one, `open-work.md` §2.13 listed them), and fail-closed that reads *"touches everything"* — `HashMap::new`, `HashMap::get`, `f64::sqrt`, `Vec::push`, `str::trim` among them. Twenty-three are `touches = []`: they compute over values the caller already holds and reach no resource in the vocabulary, which is what the file's own note beside the saturating-arithmetic entries already says about a pure computation.
- **`fs::Mapped::deref` is the one that is not**, and it gets `touches = ["file read"]` with the reason beside it: a mapping is memory that *is* a file, so reading through it reads the file — and *which* file is the mapping's rather than a parameter the line could name. An unnamed read conflicts with every file touch, which is the safe direction; `[]` would have claimed an `fs::write` to the same file may be reordered across it.
- Honest about what it changes today: **the ordering pass does not yet reduce a method call to a ledger key**, so these entries change no verdict for `"abc".len()` — measured by running the same program with and without them. They are what [ADR-075](docs/specification/adr/adr-075.md)'s build-time check will read, and `HashMap::new` is the one of the twenty-four the ordering pass can already use.

### Changed (what a program may do at compile time)

- **[ADR-075](docs/specification/adr/adr-075.md)** answers [ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) Q4, the question everything else in that record was downstream of — and it turned out to be answerable with **two columns that already exist**. Q4 asked somebody to invent a restriction; since [ADR-067](docs/specification/adr/adr-067.md), `sync` means exactly one thing and `touches` is inferred over the call graph, so the restriction is maintained by the compiler rather than by a list.
- **Counted rather than assumed.** Of `std`'s 74 `sync` entries, **ten touch something**: `cli::args` and `Args::nth` read the arguments, `print`/`println` and `eprint`/`eprintln` write, and the four lock doors take locks. So *"anything `sync`"* alone would let a build-time body read the environment it was invoked in and write into the compiler's own output — which is what makes the second column necessary and the exact boundary a decision.
- **D2: the touch set must be empty or exactly `args read`.** `cli::args` passes deliberately — same parameters, same code — because a build's parameters are an input the way its source is. `println` is refused as a *consequence of the boundary* rather than a judgement about it: a build-time print is a reasonable thing to want, `build.rs` has one and Zig has `@compileLog`, and it is a later decision.
- **D3: the determinism guarantee gains a term and the cache key gains a field.** [ADR-005](docs/specification/adr/adr-005.md) D8 is hard and CI-enforced by double-builds, and it now reads *same source, same toolchain, **same parameters***. Those are not carried today — `Choices` holds the build shape and the backend and nothing else — and its own doc comment says what to do about it: one field, one line in the key. Skipping it would make the cache serve a build made with other parameters. And `cli::args` stays one function with one meaning, *the arguments of whoever is running me*.
- **D4: no step budget for now**, with the cost written down rather than argued away: `sync` says a body never pauses, not that it **ends**, so a `const` that does not terminate hangs the compiler silently — which [ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) Q6 already called worse for the local developer than one that stops and names the call path. Deferred rather than decided against; a budget added later refuses only programs that were already hanging.
- **D6: there is no sandbox, and three questions close with it.** Q2 asked *restrict-and-interpret or permit-and-sandbox* — restricted. Q3's own table says AST interpretation is viable *only if Q2 restricts*, which it now does, and Q1 is moot without a sandbox. What goes with it: no WebAssembly runtime in the dependency tree, **no `wasm32` target required of every contributor and every CI job**, and no asterisk on determinism for NaN bit patterns, which Q3 flagged as able to make two builds both correct and different.
- **[ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) stays Open for one thing: memory.** Neither column bounds allocation, and Q4 named allocation as a requirement rather than an option. It is the mildest of the three worries it arrived with — a build that exhausts memory fails loudly, one that reads a file quietly does not, one that hangs says nothing at all.
- **And the gap that holds it up in practice**, now on [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) §2.13: **24 of those 74 `sync` entries have no touch answer at all**, so fail-closed they read *"touches everything"* — `HashMap::new`, `HashMap::get`, `f64::sqrt` among them. Under D2 a `const` could not use a map. Twenty-four missing lines, not a design problem; the inference cannot fill them because it walks Nikaia bodies and these are backed by Rust.

### Added (generics are built, and a type parameter means two things)

- **[ADR-074](docs/specification/adr/adr-074.md).** Part I 4.6 has written `struct Box[T]` since it was written, and the language had the form without the feature: the `[T]` was **read by the parser, erased by the checker and dropped by the emitter**, whose function header had no slot for one. So `fn hand[T](x: T) -> T` passed every stage of this compiler and `rustc` answered *"cannot find type `T` in this scope"* about a file nobody wrote — [Part III C.1](docs/specification/30-nikaia-tooling.md)'s class exactly.
- **D1 is the whole of it: inside its own body a type parameter is a type, and at a call site it is a variable.** [ADR-024](docs/specification/adr/adr-024.md) D4 erased `T` to `?` for a good reason — a `T` that survives into a comparison makes the checker tell a *caller* that its perfectly good `i64` is not `T` — and applied that reason to both places a parameter is read. It holds in one of them. In the body the caller has already picked what `T` is, so a body that puts an `i64` where its caller's `T` is wanted is wrong, and saying so is the only true thing to say. `Self` keeps the erasure, because no call site binds it.
- **D2: a variable binds from the arguments, and from a struct literal's fields.** [ADR-031](docs/specification/adr/adr-031.md) bound from the **receiver** and said so on purpose; a free function has none, so `hand(n)` used to be `?`. The same `ty::bind` against one pattern per argument answers `i64`, and `Pair { first: n, second: n }` is a `Pair[i64]` read back off its fields. It stays one structural walk — no queue, no fixpoint, no occurs check — because a parameter is **written, never inferred**, so there is never a variable on the right-hand side to solve for.
- **D5, `NK1126`, is what makes writing the `<T>` worth anything.** Emitting the parameter alone turns *"every generic fails"* into *"every generic whose body uses its parameter fails"*, which is the same C.1 class one size smaller: `fn shout[T](x: T) -> String { return x.to_uppercase() }` still goes to the backend. It is refused here instead — *"`T` stands for a type the caller picks, and nothing says it has a method `to_uppercase`"* — for a field and for a method alike. It says *no bound* rather than *no such method* because the method may exist on every type a caller will ever pass, and what is missing is the sentence that says so.
- **D4: an `impl`'s parameters come from the slots it already writes.** `impl Stack[T]` declares `T`; `impl Stack[i64]` declares nothing and applies `i64`; what tells them apart is whether the slot names a type. No `impl[T]` spelling is added, because Part I does not have one — and the rule lives in one function the ledger, the checker and the emitter all call, since two copies would be one silent disagreement about the names a method's signature was recorded with.
- **The ledger's `signature` column records `$T` where it recorded `?`**, which is what lets a reader see that `hand`'s result is its argument.
- **What it costs, measured rather than estimated, because it was asked before the work started.** At **run time: nothing** — Rust monomorphises, and four million calls through a generic took 137.5 ms against 139.5 ms through its concrete twins, a difference that goes both ways across runs. At **`rustc`: less than writing the copies out** — sixty functions at eight types each compiled in 273 ms as one generic and 304 ms written by hand, within 2 % on object size; what costs 2.3× in time and 5.9× in code is having 480 bodies at all, which is the author's instantiation and not genericity's overhead. In **this compiler: nothing measurable** — the front end on the three largest examples is unchanged within noise, because the work added is one `bind` per call against a signature already in hand. The figure that would change that is the one nobody should pay: inference. ADR-074 §5 has the tables.
- **What is not built, and why it is a prerequisite rather than an omission:** a **bound**. `[T: Summarize]` names a trait, and Part I 4.7's `trait Summarize { … }` is a parse error — a trait can be implemented and not declared. Until it can, a generic body may move and pass its value and nothing else, and `NK1126` says exactly that.

### Fixed (an upkeep entry that claimed a reproduction it no longer has)

- **`cargo test --workspace --release` failing the project tests does not reproduce.** It was filed with a measurement — 16 of 22 failing on `origin/main` with nothing applied, three runs out of three, against 22 of 22 passing when the same tests run alone. Seven consecutive clean whole-workspace runs since, two of them with a full rebuild immediately before in the same invocation, which was the best hypothesis and is now ruled out. The entry is **downgraded to a suspicion** rather than deleted: `open-work.md`'s own head makes that difference load-bearing, and what the earlier observation is still worth is what it rules out if the failure returns — not the tests, not the wrapper's code path, not a stale binary.

### Added (`const` is reserved, and what it will mean is decided)

- **[ADR-073](docs/specification/adr/adr-073.md).** Three records ended at the same missing word: Part II 10.2 specifies compile-time parsing and its own status note said the half it specifies cannot be written; Part I 9.2 lists **Constants** among the items `pub` applies to and carried a parenthesis saying the declaration has no syntax; [ADR-072](docs/specification/adr/adr-072.md) decides how a compile-time read is permitted and had nowhere to put the `from` line.
- **D3 is why the word earns its place, and it is not what it looks like.** The compiler **already folds constants** — a `let` bound to `2 * 3` is folded twice on the way through, and a name that folded keeps the type it took ([ADR-063](docs/specification/adr/adr-063.md)). So `const` adds no folding. It adds a **demand**: *a `let` may fold; a `const` must, and says so where it cannot.* A guarantee that silently degrades into *"it happened to be cheap"* is the kind of promise this project treats as a defect.
- **D5 stages what may stand in the initialiser and does not answer [ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) Q4.** Literals and arithmetic over them are buildable today on the fold that already exists; a call — and with it `dsl … from "…"` — waits for Q4 to say what a build-time body may do. Everything in the second group is **refused by name** rather than quietly falling back to program time, which would break D3 in exactly the way D3 is about. A `const` that parsed and evaluated arbitrary code would answer the sandbox question by accident.
- **D2** puts it where an item stands (package-private, `pub` to leave the package — Part I 9.2's existing rule, whose parenthesis this removes) and inside a body; a `const` in an `impl` is deliberately not decided. **D4** makes the type optional, inferred like a `let`'s. **D6** makes it a value and not a place: no address, no `mut`, and no second declaration for a build-time value that has one, because nothing has asked for it.
- **The example is measured rather than asserted.** A route table whose keys come from a file: the lookup is **9.0 ns against a `HashMap`'s 18.0**, five runs each on 200 keys — ×2, with the honest caveat that this is against the default hasher and [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) already picks a faster one for keys that did not come from a remote peer, so the real factor is smaller. The **3.9 µs** of startup it also saves is *not* the argument. The third thing is not a number: a malformed file stops the build instead of the program. And the line it sits past: a perfect hash over keys **in the source** is compiler-side staging and needs no `const` at all — it is the keys coming *from a file* that does.
- Built: **D1 only.** `const` is the 33rd reserved word; `const X = 1` is still two statements to the parser. Part I 2.1's table and `RESERVED_WORDS` were checked mechanically against each other again.

### Changed (a compile-time read is named three times)

- **[ADR-072](docs/specification/adr/adr-072.md).** The spelling has been in Part II 10.2 since it was written — `const CONFIG: Json::Value = dsl Json from "config.json"` — and [ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) D1 already decided that the **compiler** does that reading rather than the evaluated body. What was open was its Q5, the question with teeth: D2 keeps a build out of `/etc/passwd` and does **not** keep a dependency's grammar out of *your* `secrets.toml`, which sits inside your project root exactly where D2 allows reading.
- **D1: a build given no list reads nothing at compile time.** The strict default is what happens when nothing is passed, so *"this build reads nothing while building"* is not a claim anybody has to make, keep true, and be believed about.
- **D3 is the decision: a file must be named in all three places** — in the code as the `from "…"` literal, where a reader learns what the bytes are *for*; in an allowlist file, where a reviewer sees the whole set without reading the code; and in the invocation, `--allow-read-from-list=…`, where whoever runs the build says the set applies. Two of the three are committed, so adding a read is a diff; the third is not, and that is what it is for — a build can be run with reading switched off without editing anything.
- **D6: the list binds the whole build, dependencies included.** A package cannot bring its own permission, so a dependency that reads a file makes its consumer write the line — which is where the consumer finds out. Stricter than resolving a path relative to the package that wrote it, and deliberately so: this is the `build.rs` attack surface [ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) Q6 names, answered by making the read impossible to add quietly rather than merely recorded after the fact. [ADR-021](docs/specification/adr/adr-021.md)'s lockfile makes a new read visible *after* it happened; this makes it impossible *before*.
- **D4 and D5 are what the property is made of**, not restrictions on top of it: the path may not be computed (or *"named in the code"* stops being decidable by looking at the line — the cost is that two targets mean two literals and two list entries), and there are no patterns (a glob is a read of something nobody named, the directory). **D7** hashes the list into the cache key, because it is a file the build read. **D8** warns about an entry nothing reads, so the list stays the size of the truth rather than decaying into *everything we ever needed*.
- **The asset dimension gets its producer**, which it has been carrying through `Key::build` with a comment saying nothing reports assets yet.
- **Nothing is built, and the reason is a prerequisite rather than an omission.** There is no `const` in the parser — at item level a parse error, in a body two statements refused as *"nothing declares `const`"* — so there is no place for a `from` line to stand and nothing to check. The word is not reserved either. What stays open is [ADR-026](docs/specification/adr/adr-026.md) Q4, what a `const` body may *compute*: this record decides where the bytes come from and nothing about what happens to them afterwards.

### Added (`break`, `continue` and `loop` are reserved)

- **[ADR-071](docs/specification/adr/adr-071.md).** 29 reserved words become 32. [ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md) D2 found, while answering a different question, that **`break` and `continue` are not in the language at all** — not in the grammar, not in the parser, not on the list — so a loop is left by its condition going false or by a `return` that leaves the whole function. Whether they join the language is a question this does not answer; whether the words stay available as *names* has a deadline, and that is what is decided.
- **The rule is [ADR-051](docs/specification/adr/adr-051.md) D1's and it is about timing rather than taste** — *reserving a word costs nothing before programs exist and breaks them afterwards, so the free moment is now* — already applied once by [ADR-050](docs/specification/adr/adr-050.md) D2, which reserved `overlap` while the grammar had no construct for it.
- **`loop` is reserved although the language decided against the keyword**, which looks inconsistent and is not: [ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md) D2 wrote down the condition under which that no reopens — the day a `break` hands back a value — and the word has to still be free on that day. The polarity is settled by [ADR-050](docs/specification/adr/adr-050.md) D7, which un-reserved `seq` and recorded why that direction is free: reserving narrows what parses and un-reserving widens it, so **a reservation is reversible and its absence is not**.
- **The grammar gets a third alternation rather than two more arms.** The reserved words are one rule split in two because the backend generates a tuple and a tuple has a width `Alt` is implemented up to; twenty-eight was over it, thirty-one is further over, and the width is what broke last time.
- No program changes: none of the three appears as a name anywhere in the tree, every occurrence is in a comment about a loop. The parse error needed nothing — [ADR-051](docs/specification/adr/adr-051.md)'s note is read off the rendered message rather than off the list, so `let loop = 1` already answers *"`loop` is a reserved word, so it is not a name"*.
- **Part I 2.1's table was missing `null`**, which the compiler has reserved all along. Checked mechanically this time: the table and `RESERVED_WORDS` now hold the same 32 words.

### Changed (no unconditional loop keyword)

- **[ADR-070](docs/specification/adr/adr-070.md).** `while true { … }` is the unconditional loop and there is no second spelling for it. Part I 3.3 carries the sentence, so the absence is a decision rather than an omission, and `loop` is withdrawn from the roadmap.
- **Go is the precedent, read carefully.** Go has no `while` **at all**: `for { … }` *is* its unconditional loop. So Go did not do without the form — it did without a *second keyword* for it, by letting one word carry all three shapes. That is the lesson, and this applies it with the other word as the general one.
- **Rust's case for a `loop` keyword does not reach here.** `loop` earns its word twice in Rust — `break value` makes it an expression, and the checker knows it diverges. Neither half applies: **`break` and `continue` are not in the grammar, not in the parser, and not among the 29 reserved words**, so a loop is left by its condition or by `return` leaving the whole function, and there is no value for a `break` to carry. The day that changes is the day to reopen this, because `while true` with a value-carrying `break` reads as a lie.
- **The divergence half is a checker improvement, not a keyword**, and it is the one real cost of saying no.
