- **The preamble that makes `std`'s names resolve was written only where the program had a `use std::…` of its own** — which is not the same question (`docs/open-work.md` §1.4, the other half of the line fixed earlier). `HashMap` is a name the prelude provides and a program may write it without importing anything; such a program lowered to a file where `TrustedMap` — the name a trusted input's map gets ([ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D5) — was undeclared, and `rustc` said so about a file nobody wrote.

A suspicion nobody can reproduce is a claim about this compiler that nothing supports.

- **The preamble that makes `std`'s names resolve was written only where the program had a `use std::…` of its own** — which is not the same question (`docs/open-work.md` §1.4, the other half of the rule fixed earlier). `HashMap` is a name the prelude provides and a program may write it without importing anything; such a program lowered to a file where `TrustedMap` — the name a trusted input's map gets ([ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D5) — was undeclared, and `rustc` said so about a file nobody wrote.
- **It is written always now**, with the `#[allow(unused_imports)]` the same rule already asked for: what a program uses is not a list the emitter keeps, and it does not need one — the import is one line, and a locally declared name shadows a glob. `std` itself is the one exception, because it *is* the prelude; that is `emit::emit_std`, which says so rather than being detected.

A suspicion nobody can reproduce is a claim about this compiler that nothing supports.

### Fixed (a refusal carries no backtrace, wherever in the compiler it is made)

- **The first pass covered where a refusal usually comes from, not where one can come from** (`docs/open-work.md` §1.3). Four more modules were still handing the user `Error:` and ten frames of this compiler: the emitter refusing a `dsl` that names a grammar nobody has, the manifest reader refusing an unknown `[build]` key, the project driver refusing a missing `src/main.nika`, and the CLI refusing a backend nobody has.
- **The choice at each site is the whole decision** — is this a statement about the program, or a failure of this compiler? — so it is now visible at a glance rather than by unpicking a `format!`: `refused!` is written where `anyhow!` was and `refuse!` where `bail!` was, mirroring that pair exactly so a call site changes by one word.
- **Four sites keep their trace on purpose**: two emitter invariants, the `rustc` wrapper invoked with no compiler named, and reading the sysroot's own files. Those are this compiler's problems, and the frames are then the most useful thing on the screen. A test drives four different modules through the CLI with `RUST_BACKTRACE=1` set — which is the case the rule is about — because what is being tested is that the rule is not one path's habit.

### Changed (a length is an `i64`, and the numeric surface is the one the page names)

- **[ADR-048](docs/specification/adr/adr-048.md) D1 is built: `xs.len()` hands back an `i64`, and an index takes one.** The two conversions are not symmetric, which is the whole argument. Out of a length cannot fail — a `usize` exceeds an `i64` only above eight exabytes of single-byte elements — and the user wrote it in every loop, sum and comparison that met a length. Into an index can fail, for a negative number, but **a negative index is an access out of bounds**, which Part III A.2 already aborts on: not a new failure mode, the same one a step earlier. So the trade is a great deal of visible ceremony that cannot fail against one invisible abort that already exists — and the user now writes no conversion at all, in either direction.
- **Both conversions are emitted.** `xs.len()` becomes `xs.len() as i64`; an index becomes `xs[nikaia_std::index::at(i)]`, where `at` chooses on the **type** of what is in the brackets — an integer becomes a `usize`, and anything else, a map's key most of all, goes through untouched. It has to be a trait and not a function because this emitter does not know types ([ADR-011](docs/specification/adr/adr-011.md) D2) and `counts[path]` is an index too. A negative index panics as *"index out of bounds: the index is -1"*, which D1 requires: `as usize` would have said 18446744073709551613 instead, a number nothing in the program contains.
- **An index written only in literals is left alone.** `xs[0]` and `&text[1..3]` need no conversion — Rust's own inference gives a literal the `usize` a sequence wants — and they cannot take one: `at(0)` has nothing to infer its argument type from, and `cannot infer type` about a generated file is what Part III C.1 forbids.
- **The machine-width type leaves the writable surface**, and `usize::truncating_i32` and `usize::truncating_i64` go with it: nothing narrows out of a type nobody can hold. `check`'s `NUMERIC` — the list that decides which conversions are checked at run time — is now `i32, i64, f64, u8`; the machine-width types were in it *because* `len` handed one back. `docs/open-work.md` §2.7 is gone with them, the question removed rather than answered.
- **`u8` is named in Part I 2.2** (D2), with the same conversion and arithmetic names as every other numeric type: `fs::read` hands back a `Vec[u8]`, so a program that reads a file already met one and what was missing was the specification admitting it. `u32`, `u64` and `isize` stay out until a program asks. The page now also says which types the compiler accepts without offering, so the list of types is the list of types.
- **The parentheses around a conversion are decided in one place**, which this change forced and [ADR-043](docs/specification/adr/adr-043.md) D7 needed already. Three things come out as a Rust `as`: a written cast, `truncating_i32`, and a length. Rust reads such an expression wrongly in exactly two positions — beside a unary operator (`-x as i64` is `(-x) as i64`) and as the **left of a comparison**, where `x as i64 < k` is read as `i64<k>` and the program does not parse — so parentheses go there and nowhere else. `truncating_` used to parenthesise always, which is the other half of C.1: `let n = (big as i32);` is a Rust warning about a file nobody wrote.

### Fixed (the compiled-`std` cache stops filling the disk)

- **A cache with no eviction is a disk leak** (`docs/open-work.md` §1.8). The compiled-`std` tree is a whole Cargo target directory of some 240 MB, and the key naming it holds the compiler's own fingerprint — so every rebuild of the compiler started a new one and nothing ever took an old one away. Measured: 1.7 GB in one user cache, **13 GB** in the directory the project tests share, and a build failing on "no space left on device". Coexisting is right ([ADR-021](docs/specification/adr/adr-021.md) D7 — dimensions coexist, they do not share); coexisting *forever* was the defect, now recorded as D12.5.
- **The newest three are kept and idle ones below that are removed.** Three because switching target or codegen table back and forth is a normal thing to do and evicting on every switch turns a cache into a tax. An hour's age floor sits on top of the count so that the tree a **concurrent** build is writing into is never the one that goes — and a build marks its own tree in use *before* sweeping, so it can never collect itself. Ordered by a marker file rather than the directory's own timestamp, because Cargo writes into subdirectories and leaves the top alone. A sweep that fails is a fuller disk and never a failed build, which is D12's rule already.

### Fixed (the explain modes reach a project, and two more shared counts stopped diverging)

- **`--sharing`, `--overlaps` and `--trust` work on `nikaia build` and `nikaia run`** (`docs/open-work.md` §1.7). They were on the single-file path only — `nikaia build --sharing` answered `unexpected argument` — which is exactly where the asking is not done: `--sharing` exists because there is no way to *request* the cheaper reference count, every fallback is enumerated instead, and *"that is only fair if the fallbacks can be asked about. This is the asking."* A person with a real program builds it with `nikaia build`. They report over every file of the package, against the package's own ledger rather than each file's, and one function serves both paths so the two cannot disagree.
- **A `Shared` handed to a public parameter of a function another file declares kept two counts**, exactly as a field did — found by a test written for the flags above. The floor is therefore about **any slot another file owns**, not about fields, and it is read off the union-find rather than off the recorded handles: a slot another file owns has no handle in this run, and joining to it is the only trace of it there is. `Fallback::ForeignField` is `Fallback::ForeignFile`.
- **And the sharing analysis did not walk into an interpolated string.** `println(f"{hold(c)}")` handed a handle to a function the analysis never saw, so nothing joined and nothing forced. A hole is Nikaia source ([ADR-032](docs/specification/adr/adr-032.md) D3) and the type checker has walked holes since that record; any analysis that stops at a literal is one a hole can be hidden in.

### Changed (a package is a directory, and its files are one namespace)

- **[ADR-047](docs/specification/adr/adr-047.md) D1 is built.** Every `.nika` beside the entry takes part, whether or not anything names it, and they share one namespace: no `use` between them, no prefix on the names they share. The emitted Rust is **one crate root** with every file's items in it — no `mod`, no `use super::*`, nothing to resolve, because one namespace here is one namespace in the language below. The entry goes first, being the file that may declare `main`, and the rest in file-name order, so the output is a function of the tree rather than of a directory listing (Part III 13.5).
- **Two files declaring the same name is refused here, naming both files.** One namespace, so it is an error rather than a rule about which of them a line means — and `rustc` would report it as a duplicate definition against a file nobody wrote (Part III, C.1).
- **A `use` is refused, and which mistake it is decides the message.** One that names a file beside this one says the files of a package already see one another and to remove the line, because the shape that used to work is the one most likely to be written; one that names anything else says `use` names another package and that depending on one is not built. `use std::…` is untouched.
- **Privacy moved with the boundary**, so a name that is not `pub` is visible throughout its package — and `NK1110` therefore fires on nothing at all. The check is built and the boundary moved out from under it: a qualified name is a name from another package, and depending on one is not built. It is the one code in Part III C.3's catalogue with a check behind it and no program that reaches it, and the day a package arrives is the day `pub` starts to mean something a program can observe.
- **Outside a project the entry is compiled on its own.** A package is a directory *of a project*, which is what a `nikaia.toml` declares; `examples/` is a directory of eleven programs that each declare `main`, and compiling one must not pull in the other ten.
- **`examples/inventory` is the change made visible.** It was this repository's first program of more than one file, written with `use stock` and `stock::total(…)`; it is three files of one package with neither. The comments explaining what a module boundary bought were rewritten rather than kept as an account of a rule that is gone.

### Added (two decisions get their records: what `use` does, and what a package is)

- **[ADR-046](docs/specification/adr/adr-046.md): `use` makes a unit of code reachable and brings no name in** — not by a glob, not by a braced list, not one at a time. The blanket form is regretted everywhere it exists and settles itself; the selected form is the real question, and it is refused on a different ground: it is everywhere and withdrawn nowhere, so it is not a mistake. What decides it is a rule this language has already applied to itself — **one form, not two spellings of it** ([ADR-041](docs/specification/adr/adr-041.md) withdrew the automatic lambda argument names on exactly that ground). A selected import creates that situation for every name in the language. And the refusal is not irreversible, which is why it can be firm: adding the form later breaks no program, while a question left open is one every file written in the meantime has to live with unanswered. `use x as y` shortens a long prefix, a prefix must be introduced, and one name per file.
- **[ADR-047](docs/specification/adr/adr-047.md): a package is a directory, and its files share one namespace.** No `use` between them; what the package offers outward is whatever says `pub`, in whichever file it is declared. This removes the re-export problem instead of patching it — there is nothing to re-offer, because the names already share a space — and a library's internal file layout stops being its public surface by construction. Java, Go and Rust all put that boundary above the single file; Python puts it at the file and `__init__.py` is the consequence. Part I 9.2's privacy boundary moves from the file to the package, and two files of one package may not declare the same name.
- **A library arrives by path**, the way `std` already does, so this generalises a mechanism rather than adding one. No registry, no name space, no distribution format — the three [ADR-002](docs/specification/adr/adr-002.md) D1 §5 refuses to guess at stay open. Five rules come with it or they get decided by accident, and the fifth is the one that would have been decided wrongly by silence: a Nikaia dependency is part of the **program** and not a foreign package, so [ADR-043](docs/specification/adr/adr-043.md)'s overflow check reaches it — otherwise a library computes silently wrong numbers while the program calling it aborts.
- **Part I 9.1 and 9.2 are rewritten** for both, each with a Status note saying what the compiler actually enforces today: the unit is still the file, and `NK1110` still says *"private to `utils.nika`"*. The half of the machinery a package boundary needs is built one level down — a qualified name from another unit resolves to one type.

### Fixed (a module can hand out types, and a shared field agrees across files)

- **A qualified type name resolved to a different type from the one the call handed back** (`docs/open-work.md` §1.1). `let c: pool::Conn = pool::make()` was `NK1103`, with a help line asking for exactly what was already written — a module could hand out behaviour and not data, which is most of the reason to split a program. One spelling was missing: the ledger keys a type `pool::Conn`, while a module's own signature says `-> Conn` because that is how the file declaring it writes the name. `Ledger::absorb` now qualifies the types **inside** an entry as well as its key — it is the only place that knows both the module and what that module declares — so the two spellings are one name before anything compares them.
- **And a struct from another file could not be built at all**: `pool::Conn(id: 1)` was a parse error, because the literal's name was parsed as `NAME` where a type's is `type_name`. Both are `type_name` now, and a foreign struct's fields are read from the program's own ledger by the **exact** key — never by suffix, which is how `std` is matched: two modules may each declare a `Conn`, and a suffix match would answer with whichever came first. A wrong field name, a wrong field type and a wrong annotation are now all refused by their qualified names.
- **A `Shared` in a field another file declares kept two different counts** (§1.6), and fixing the above is what uncovered it: the analysis runs once per file, so `pool.nika` saw the field and not the value while `main.nika` saw the value and not the field. Measured, in one generated file: `pub db: std::sync::Arc<Conn>` beside `let c: std::rc::Rc<pool::Conn>`, which `rustc` refused — about a file nobody wrote. The answer is the polarity this analysis already runs on rather than a new rule: where it cannot prove that nothing crosses, it does not lower. Such a field keeps the atomic floor as `Fallback::ForeignField`, a seventh row in the enumeration `--sharing` prints, because a fallback nobody can ask about is not a fallback this design allows.
- **Forced in one place**, after the walk and before the classes are read off, rather than at each of the three sites that create a field slot: a rule that has to be remembered at three sites is one that will be forgotten at the fourth. The declaring file forces the same field where it is public, so the two runs agree by both refusing to lower; where it does not, the other file cannot name the type either and the caution costs nothing.

### Fixed (a word this language does not know, and what leaves the compiler when a program is wrong)

- **A word keyword matched the beginning of a longer word, and the program that came out was a different one.** The grammar is scannerless (ADR-001 D2), so nothing said where a word ends: measured, `true asfoo` read as `true as foo`, `returnx` as `return x`, `assert c` as `let c = true as sert; c;` — the cast took `sert` for a type name — and `forx in 0..3` as a **loop that binds `x`**, which is a valid program with a different meaning from the one written. Every word keyword now carries a boundary (`parser::KW_*`): one rule per keyword because the generator has no parameters, and UPPERCASE, which is not a style choice — a lowercase rule is syntactic, so the implicit whitespace would go *between* the word and its boundary and `as i32` would be refused for having a space in it.
- **And a statement that is one undeclared name is `NK1117`**, in this language's words. That is what was left of the class: `assert`, `unsafe` and the `_000` of `1_000` are read as names, a name on its own is a legal statement, and each of them used to lower and be refused by `rustc` about a file nobody wrote (Part III, C.1). Part I 2.2 is deliberate that `1_000` is `1` beside a name — the reading is right, being *accepted* was not. Four things count as declaring a name, and anything the compiler cannot see is a name it does not refuse, because refusing a correct program is the one thing it may never do (C.4).
- **An automatic lambda argument is now declared where the checker can see it.** `fn { … }` has no parameter list, so its body walked with `a` in scope nowhere — harmless while nothing asked whether a name is declared, and a false refusal the moment `NK1117` does. The answer comes from `emit::implicit_params`, the same function the emitter writes the parameter list from, so the two cannot disagree about it.
- **A refused program leaves without a backtrace.** A type error ended as an `anyhow::Error` out of `main`, so after the diagnostics had said their piece the terminal also got `Error: 1 type error` — and where `RUST_BACKTRACE` is set, which is a normal thing for a developer to have set, ten frames of `nikaia::project::check` and below. `diagnostics::Refused` marks a statement about the *program*; the tally stays and the envelope goes. A parse error travels the same path, which needed one more fix: `modules.rs` formatted the error into a string to prefix the file name, and formatting loses the type that says it is a refusal.
- **A failure of this compiler keeps its trace**, deliberately — a file it cannot read is its own problem, and the frames are then the most useful thing on the screen. A test asserts that half too, or the change would be indistinguishable from one that swallowed everything.
- **No Rust warning about the generated file reaches the user.** Every multi-file build printed `unused import: super::*` for a `use` item this compiler writes itself, and the translation recognised it, said *"no Nikaia source maps to this"*, and printed it anyway — telling the reader to remove a line they never wrote. Both halves are fixed because either alone leaves the hole: the emitted import carries `#[allow(unused_imports)]`, and a **warning** that maps to no Nikaia line is not reported at all. An **error** with nowhere to put it still is: a warning suppressed costs nothing, an error suppressed leaves a build that failed with no reason given anywhere.

### Changed (the crossing verdict takes the destination, so a lock may reach a task)

- **The question is asked of a value *and a destination*** ([ADR-045](docs/specification/adr/adr-045.md) D1). It used to be asked of the type alone, and for a lock that meant taking the worse of the two settings of `user_parallelism` and answering "may not" everywhere — so **Part II 12.2's counter, the program `user_parallelism = yes` exists to serve, could not be written**. At `yes` a real operating-system lock stands in the emitted program, under which that counter is entirely safe, and it was refused on account of an implementation that does not occur in it. There are two destinations now and each gets an answer that is the same at both settings, which is the whole of what [ADR-005](docs/specification/adr/adr-005.md) §1 Group B asks: the justification may differ per setting, the answer may not.
- **Into a task of your own a lock may go, at both settings** (D2) — at `yes` because a real lock is underneath and several threads are what it is for, at `no` because the task is interleaved on the same thread and nothing crosses at all. Two reasons, one answer. `Locked[T]` and `SharedMut[T]` are answered by what they hold there, like a container: a lock makes nothing crossable that was not, which is Group B's transitivity rather than an exception to it.
- **Into code nothing written down describes it may not, at both settings — and deliberately the worse answer** (D3). Strictly it would be safe at `yes`; it is refused anyway, because a lock's Rust type differs per setting while a Rust library has one signature, so a library written against one would compile at one setting and fail at the other. The message says the answer was *chosen*, so that somebody tripping over it in two years can see a decision behind it rather than an oversight — and the way out is not "don't do that": the caller opens the lock and hands the **value inside it** over, which is the shape an ordinary function already has ([ADR-042](docs/specification/adr/adr-042.md) D1).
- **`NK2501` and `NK2502` stop sharing one verdict**, which ends [ADR-037](docs/specification/adr/adr-037.md) D6's coda: it said that after D6 no type answers "may not", and the lock answers it at a foreign call. So `NK2502` has an occupant and Part III C.5's *"built, and no program reaches it"* goes with it, while `NK2501` refuses nothing a Nikaia program can write.
- **The third call site is `Ours`, and this record did not decide that for it.** `contracts::order` asks the same verdict about a signature's result, because overlapping two statements puts each inside a closure that hands its value back. That closure is this compiler's own, on this compiler's own thread, so it is the destination a task is — and a pair whose result holds a lock overlaps now where it kept its place before.
- **The debt `contracts::send`'s own header wrote down came due, and the second column turned out to be the destination.** The header required every name in its tables to be safe both to *move* to another thread and to be *looked at* from one, and said that the day a name belonged in one column and not the other the file would need two. A lock is exactly that name — at `no` it may be moved and may not be looked at — and it never has to be asked that way, because both destinations answer before the distinction matters. The columns stay one; the table splits by destination.
- **The type is still not built**, so this is a verdict ahead of its construct: neither `SharedMut[T]` nor `Locked[T]` is a type the backend can lower ([ADR-039](docs/specification/adr/adr-039.md) §4), and a program that writes one as an annotation is checked here and then fails to emit. The verdict is the half worth having early — it is what a library's signature is written against, and it is what 12.2's counter was blocked on.

### Added (a response may be bytes the program never allocated, and the criticism that asked for it was measured)

- **A body need not be a value the program owns a copy of** ([ADR-058](docs/specification/adr/adr-058.md) D1, [#45](https://github.com/keywan-ghadami/Nikaia/pull/45)). [ADR-018](docs/specification/adr/adr-018.md) D2's table had three rows and every one of them was bytes the handler was holding, so the only way to answer with a file was to read it — which is what the README's server did, once per request. `Bytes` joins the table, `Mapped` derefs to it, and a page mapped **once outside the handler** is a body that costs a reference count per request. The handler answering with it duplicates the handle rather than moving it, which is [ADR-040](docs/specification/adr/adr-040.md) D1's rule applied to the one handle that record did not name — without it a captured body would answer exactly one request.
- **And a response may be a file the program never read** (D2): `http::File("index.html")`, whose bytes go from the page cache to the socket without entering the process. It is a *response body* and not a file handle — `std::fs` keeps the surface [ADR-019](docs/specification/adr/adr-019.md) argued for and gains nothing. Its length and any failure to open it are settled before the status line, because after the headers are written there is no status code left to send (D6).
- **Which mechanism carries the bytes is `std`'s choice and not the program's** (D3), and the argument for it is prior art rather than this project's benchmark. Apache httpd's `EnableSendfile` and nginx's `sendfile` are **off by default** — Apache's having been *on* in 2.2, so a project with two decades of deployments moved that default the other way; nginx's `sendfile_max_chunk` bounds one call for fairness rather than throughput; HAProxy's kernel splicing is opt-in with a fallback to an ordinary copy; lighttpd's `server.network-backend` names the mechanism as a configuration value. Not one of the four puts it in what an application writes. [ADR-038](docs/specification/adr/adr-038.md) D5's runtime file gains `send-file`, default `auto` and pinnable — the shape all four arrived at.
- **And the benchmark that shaped the first draft was re-run four more times, which retired three of its conclusions.** Absolutes move 2× between runs of the same binary on the same four cores. *"`sendfile` loses at 64 KiB"* was one busy afternoon of five runs — in the other four it is the best vehicle in its family there. *"The crossover is near a megabyte"*: there is none to find on a quiet box. *"`splice` is worst at every size"*: at a megabyte it is mid-field. What reproduced in all five is what the record now rests on — reading per request is worst everywhere, **`mmap` per request is 2–3× worse than plainly reading** below a megabyte, a kept mapping wins at 4 KiB by ~2×, and `sendfile` is never worse than reading. `docs/zero-copy-send.md` §2.1 lists what was retired and §5 the prior art, with its sources.
- **`io_uring`'s `Splice` is not the mechanism, and the number that says so is one an easy benchmark would have got backwards** (D4), at the two sizes where it reproduced. A pipe has to stand between a file and a socket, which is a second transfer that no batching removes: **78, 89 and 955 µs** of machine CPU against every other vehicle's less, with both hops submitted as one linked pair and the pipe widened first. Its *sending thread* meanwhile pays a flat ~21 µs at every size, because the work is done by kernel workers no per-thread accounting sees — so the losing mechanism looks like the winner by a factor of twenty on the instrument most benchmarks reach for. The machine's busy time is the column this record is stated in.
- **TLS and HTTP/2 turn D2 off as a property rather than a fallback** (D5). `rustls` takes bytes and produces bytes ([ADR-038](docs/specification/adr/adr-038.md) D2), and bytes that never enter the process cannot be encrypted; HTTP/2 interleaves DATA frames, so there is no single run to hand the kernel. Both serve the file through D1's mapped path instead, and kTLS is named as unbuilt rather than promised. On `wasm32-unknown` there is neither `sendfile` nor a mapping, so the file is read — the surface is the same and the mechanism is the target's.
- **Nothing of it is built, and one part is deliberately not.** `std::http` does not exist ([ADR-038](docs/specification/adr/adr-038.md) §4.5), so neither the row nor `http::File` has anywhere to be; the order that record gives is unchanged — a socket layer that keeps registrations, then a minimal HTTP/1.1 server, then the parsing moved into Nikaia, then `rustls`, then HTTP/2. A `send_file` in `nikaia_std::rt` *could* be built ahead of all of it and is not: it is the mechanism that loses at the sizes a server sends most.
- **There are two programs, and the answer is not the same one** ([ADR-058](docs/specification/adr/adr-058.md) D3). A page whose path is a constant may do everything once outside the handler; a route whose file the *request* names — a download, a file server, an upload served back — may do nothing once. Measured with the requests cycling over 64 files rather than repeating one: **`mmap` per request costs 76.0 µs of machine CPU at 4 KiB against a plain read's 30.5**, two and a half times worse, because a mapping is a page-table edit and not a pointer. So D3 forbids the obvious reading of D1: `std` may not implement `http::File` by mapping per request. Where nothing may be kept, `sendfile` is the mechanism — 28.0 µs at 4 KiB and 49.5 at 64 KiB, best or tied at every size, and the only one whose cost does not include a copy into the process.
- **What wins is not a mechanism at all** (D8). A table of mappings made once, looked up by path, is **13.5 µs** where the best keeping-nothing vehicle is 28.0. So `std` keeps one — bounded (`static-cache` in [ADR-038](docs/specification/adr/adr-038.md) D5's runtime file, `0` disables it), dropped when the file's identity or modification time moves, which is a correctness property and therefore has no key that turns it off, and never a promise a program may depend on. A page that must be held for certain is mapped by the program itself, outside the handler, which is D1 and needs none of this.
- **A path out of a request is `Untrusted` and may not reach the filesystem unchecked** (D7). [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D8 built a taint lattice and named three consumers needing their own decision: hashing was D5 of that record, escaping was [ADR-017](docs/specification/adr/adr-017.md), and **path traversal** was the third and open one. Handing `request.query("file")` to `http::File`, `fs::map`, `fs::read` or `fs::write` is a **compile error** rather than a runtime check — a `../../etc/shadow` that arrives as a 200 is not a failure a status code fixes afterwards. What clears it is `fs::within(root, name)`, whose answer is trusted where its argument was not and is `none` where the join would leave the root, or `trusted: true` at the source with its site in `nikaia.contracts`. It needed **no new analysis**: the taint travels the `join` that is exactly how a traversal bug is written, because [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D1's lattice already rides [ADR-008](docs/specification/adr/adr-008.md)'s edges — which is the argument D8 made for building the mechanism generally, arriving as evidence.
- **D7 is the one piece worth building before the server, and it is named as such.** It needs no socket: `contracts::trust` exists and `nikaia --trust` prints what it found; what is missing is a diagnostic where an untrusted value reaches a path parameter, and `fs::within` beside it. Testable against `fs::map` today, and every program that later writes `http::File` inherits it.
- **Two hazards the benchmark structurally cannot see, from the prior art** (D9). A mapped file another writer **truncates faults the serving process with a bus error** — Apache documents it for NFS and recommends `EnableMMAP off` there — which no invalidation check can prevent, because the fault arrives first; so D8 gains a fourth property and keeps a mapping only under a root the operator named, which is where D7's `fs::within` already puts one. And **`sendfile` blocks on a cold page cache**: nginx needed `SF_NODISKIO` and a thread pool to keep that off a worker, and at `user_parallelism = no` there is one thread for everything the user wrote, so a blocking call there is not a slow request but every other connection stopped. Named now so that whoever builds D3 does not find it in production.
- **`benches/sendfile/` is a workspace member and [`docs/zero-copy-send.md`](docs/zero-copy-send.md) is the write-up** — the method, the machine, the spread, and the four things it does **not** measure, named rather than folded into the recommendation: a slow far-away client (where `sendfile` needs a descriptor and the alternatives need the body addressable per connection, which is a memory argument this bench cannot see), an encrypted connection, a cold page cache, and a cache that evicts — the bench's keeps all 64 mappings forever and never asks whether the file changed, which is the correctness work D8 prices and it does not. The whole block was run twice and every ordering reproduced except the megabyte row of the second family, where three of four vehicles sit inside each other's spread; no claim rests on it beyond the kept mapping winning, which both runs agree on.

### Fixed (the README's code example is refused by this compiler)

- **A specification example wrote a postfix `??` the language does not have**, found by extracting Part III 17.1's own download route and handing it to the compiler. Part I 3.5 defines `??` as null coalescing — `a ?? b` — and nothing else; `lookup(q??)` is `expected expression; found ')'`. It is not a rule specified ahead of the compiler, because those carry a Status note and this has none: it is an example using a construct no section defines, which a reader cannot look up when it fails. 17.1's example is rewritten to use what 3.5 has; [ADR-018](docs/specification/adr/adr-018.md) D3 writes the same form and **stands**, because an ADR is written once and the correction belongs to whatever decides whether the nullable gets a postfix unwrap at all. [`open-work.md`](docs/open-work.md) §3.7 is that question, and `spec-promises.md` gains the probe.

- **The flagship example did not parse** ([#45](https://github.com/keywan-ghadami/Nikaia/pull/45)). `fn handle_request(req: http::Request) throws IoError` names an error type, which [ADR-023](docs/specification/adr/adr-023.md) D1 forbids and which the parser rejects by name — the one program in this repository most people read, and it was never run through the compiler it advertises. Three more: `req.respond(200, data)` is not surface ([ADR-018](docs/specification/adr/adr-018.md) D2 has a handler *return* what answers the request), the file reader is `fs::read_to_string`, and `.route("/", handler)` is the form the trailing lambda of [ADR-022](docs/specification/adr/adr-022.md) replaced. What is there now is Part III 17.1's own server with a query parameter added, and it lowers.
- **The absence list goes with it.** "Note what is *absent*: no `async`, no `await`, no `.unwrap()`, no lifetimes, no `Arc::clone`" describes other languages, and a comment explaining `spawn`'s polymorphism explained a runtime that does not exist. What replaces both is two properties stated positively and a status note saying `std::http` is not built. The example count below it was stale too: eleven of twelve run, and `inventory/` was missing from the list.

### Fixed (an overflow aborts at every build, and so does a conversion that does not fit)

- **The generated project named one profile, so a program aborted on an `i32` overflow while every benchmark here runs `--release`, where it wraps** ([ADR-043](docs/specification/adr/adr-043.md) D1, D6). The measurements were taken on wrapping arithmetic and the shipped program aborted — two different programs, and nobody chose that. The specification was silent on overflow, which is a gap and not a contradiction: Part III A.2 already had the category (*"errors indicating an inconsistent program state"*) and an overflow is the same kind of thing as the division by zero beside it. A.2's list gains it, so Part I 1.2's rule — you choose *how* when you build, never *what* — now holds for arithmetic too.
- **The check is carried by the generated `Cargo.toml`, on for the program and off for every dependency.** Not by a checked helper per operation, which was considered and refused: the arithmetic is identical either way, but anyone who opens the emitted Rust during a benchmark comparison and finds a helper call where `+` should be will not believe the numbers. A dependency's hash function wraps on purpose and is not this compiler's code to be right about, so the rule stops at the crate boundary. `overflow-checks` is deliberately **not** in `Profile`, which holds what the build switches and the codegen table decide; an overflow aborting is what the language *means*, so it is written unconditionally and there is no key that turns it off.
- **Three tests, because no single one sees the whole of it** (`crates/nikaia/tests/overflow.rs`): the emitted Rust aborts with the check on, and says `a + b`; the manifest this compiler writes for a real project carries both halves; and — `#[ignore]`d, like every Cargo-driven test here — a `nikaia run` of an overflowing program aborts end to end. Run once to confirm the seam: it does.
- **Part I 2.2 gains what the area never had.** The abort and the three cases that look harmless (negating or taking the absolute value of the smallest signed value, the smallest divided by `-1`, shifting wider than the type); `wrapping_*` and `saturating_*` by name, with no operator for either, because a sign would have to be spelt with `&` and that is borrowing here; `as`, which turns out to be **unspecified altogether** despite being in the parser and used four times in `examples/1brc.nika`; and that a literal out of range is a compile error rather than an abort.
- **Measured while writing the Status note rather than asserted.** `5000000000 as i32` prints `705032704` — it truncates silently, so D4's check is real work and not a formality. And `let x: i32 = 3000000000` *is* already refused where it is written, but by rustc and in Rust's words, down to the lint name `overflowing_literals` and the advice to use `u32`: the position is this language's and the message is not, which is the Part III C.1 class.

- **And a conversion that does not fit aborts too, by a second mechanism** ([ADR-043](docs/specification/adr/adr-043.md) D4). The setting above cannot carry it and that is no flaw in either: Rust's `as` is *defined* to truncate, so there is no check to switch on — measured, `5000000000 as i32` prints `705032704` under `-C overflow-checks=on` and under `-C debug-assertions=on` alike. So a narrowing conversion is emitted as a checked one instead, and the test compiles with `-O` and **no** check flag at all, which is the claim: a conversion carries its own answer and takes none from the build. The objection that refused helpers for the arithmetic does not reach here — a conversion is rare and written down on purpose rather than standing in every loop, and the checked form is what a Rust programmer would write at that spot anyway.
- **Which conversions those are is the checker's answer, not the emitter's.** `as i32` narrows or widens by what it is *given*, and the emitter resolves no types ([ADR-028](docs/specification/adr/adr-028.md)) — so the checker records `(statement, target type)` where every such conversion in the statement narrows, and the emitter looks the pair up. The fourth use of that arrangement, after `fallible_loops`, `fallible_methods` and `shared_sites`. The list is written the other way round — which conversions **always** fit — because a pair nobody thought about should be checked rather than truncated in silence.
- **`truncating_i32` is how a program says it wanted the low digits** ([ADR-043](docs/specification/adr/adr-043.md) D7), after the pattern of the `wrapping_` names: the operation in the prefix, the destination in the name, because there is one method per destination and the ledger has to write a signature for each. It lowers to Rust's `as` and to nothing else — in parentheses, because `as` sits between the unary and the binary operators in Rust's precedence while a method call sits above both, so `-x.truncating_i32()` has to keep meaning `-(x as i32)`.
- **Three neighbours of the same family, so none of them arrives separately later.** Out of an `f64` the old conversion was silent three ways — `1e20 as i32` was the largest `i32`, `-1e20 as i32` the smallest, and "not a number" was `0` — and Rust offers no `try_from` for it, so `nikaia_std::num` carries the test. A **count** is as wide as the machine is, so `text.len() as i32` is checked for the reason the overflow decision exists at all: otherwise the behaviour hangs on where the program was built. An integer to an `f64` is the one that stays unchecked — `9007199254740993` through an `f64` comes back `9007199254740992`, there is no sensible place to stop, and that is written down in Part I 2.2 as a limit.
- **The range test goes through `i128`, and the obvious spelling was wrong.** Comparing against the target's limits as `f64` accepts a value one past what the type holds, because `i64::MAX as f64` rounds **up** — and for a machine-width type the constant would have to differ per machine. `f64 as i128` saturates far outside every target's range, so a saturated value cannot land back inside it and `try_from` then answers exactly, at whatever width the machine gives the type. "Not a number" is asked first, because it is the one case no range test catches: it compares false against everything and `as` turns it into `0`.
- **Two things the emitted abort leaked, both found by running it.** `.expect` appends the error's `Debug`, so the message carried `TryFromIntError(())` — Rust internals in a sentence a Nikaia user reads, the same leak the diagnostics filter exists to stop; it is a `panic!` with the sentence now. And the `std` helper's abort named `num.rs` rather than the generated line, which would leave [ADR-044](docs/specification/adr/adr-044.md)'s table nothing to translate: `#[track_caller]` fixes it, but only with the `panic!` in the function's own body — inside a closure handed to `unwrap_or_else` the attribute does not reach, measured.

### Added (a handle on a shared value is duplicated, and what it costs is readable)

- **A handle handed on by value is duplicated** ([ADR-040](docs/specification/adr/adr-040.md) D1). `keep(db)`, where `keep` takes a `Shared[Connection]`, lowers to `keep(db.clone())`, so the name outside keeps working and each handle dies at the end of its own block (D3). Unconditionally, and not only where the name is used again (D2): a line further down may not decide what a line further up does to a cleanup point. Only where the argument **names** a handle — a variable, or a field of one — because a temporary has no block of its own to die at the end of and so produces no second owner. `.clone()` rather than `Rc::clone(&x)`, because it is right under either count and so cannot disagree with the type the position was lowered to.
- **A borrow duplicates nothing**, which is D1's own correction and the half most easily got wrong. `serve(&db)` and a `&Shared[T]` parameter are both handed on as written; nothing steps the count, and the tests check both directions in one program.
- **`--sharing` names each duplication site beside the count it printed** ([ADR-040](docs/specification/adr/adr-040.md) D5). D1 leaves the place the atomic instruction is paid unwritten in the source, so it has to be readable somewhere, and the count was already printed there. A value that was only ever borrowed says so — the absence of a step is what a reader came to look up. The site names the callee and the position it takes the handle in, and not a line: an `Expr` carries no span in this AST, which is the limit `check::Checked::fallible_methods` already records about a method call.
- **A handle read out of a field and handed on by value now joins the callee's class.** `keep(pool.db)` gives the callee an owner as surely as `keep(db)` does, and the field's slot was left unjoined — so the field's class and the parameter's could disagree about which count they are, and the emitted Rust would hand an `Rc` where an `Arc` is wanted. That is a `rustc` error about a file nobody wrote, which Part III C.1 forbids, and it is the fail-open direction `docs/rc-or-arc.md` §8 warns about.
- **The task half is not built, because there is no task.** The analysis sees a `spawn` body using a handle and names it as a duplication site; `spawn` does not lower, so no emitted program reaches it (Part II, 11.2).

### Added (`Shared[T]` is a type a program can write and run)

- **`Shared[T]` exists.** `std.contracts` carries `[type."Shared"]` and one method entry, `Shared::deref` with `signature = "(&Shared[$T]) -> &$T"`; the backend lowers the type to `std::rc::Rc<T>` or `std::sync::Arc<T>`; and the annotated `let` of Part I 6.2 makes the first handle — `let db: Shared[Connection] = connect("…")` becomes `Rc::new(connect("…"))`. There is no `Shared::new` and there must not be: the annotation *is* the constructor ([ADR-040](docs/specification/adr/adr-040.md) §3). A field whose declared type says so is the other place one is made, and a call site is neither — `NK1115` refuses that and points at the line where the sharing belongs.
- **Which of the two counts a value gets is decided per value, not per type** ([ADR-037](docs/specification/adr/adr-037.md) D6 and D7). `emit::Emitter::map_name` sees only a name and could not answer it, so `contracts::sharing` computes one count per slot over the whole unit and the emitter looks it up — the third use of the arrangement `check::Checked::fallible_loops` and `fallible_methods` already have: computed where the knowledge is, looked up where the code is written. A count belongs to the **allocation**, so every position of one union-find class carries the same answer and the types on both sides of a call agree by construction; a position nothing decided gets the atomic floor.
- **`serve(&db)` works through the `deref` entry and nothing new.** [ADR-042](docs/specification/adr/adr-042.md) D2 saw a transparent container through to what it holds as soon as its ledger recorded a `deref`, and D2's own note said `Shared` would be seen through "the moment it has a `deref` entry, with no further decision". It is, and the receiver's arguments bind `$T` ([ADR-031](docs/specification/adr/adr-031.md)), so one line answers for every `Shared` a program writes. A borrow duplicates nothing, which is [ADR-040](docs/specification/adr/adr-040.md) D1's correction.
- **An annotated `let` is an allocation this analysis watched being made.** `contracts::sharing` forced such a handle to the atomic count as `Fallback::UnseenOrigin` — "it came out of a call" — which was true while the only way a `Shared` could arrive was from somewhere else, and is false of the line that makes one. It is answered yes only where something written down says the value is not already a handle: a literal, a struct literal, or a call whose ledger result type is known and holds no `Shared`. A result of `?` is the absence of a claim and is answered no, which keeps the polarity the file runs on. Without this, the base case [ADR-037](docs/specification/adr/adr-037.md) §5 asks for — a `Shared` nothing crosses with really does get the plain count — was unreachable for every program that actually writes one.
- **Verified by compiling and running, not by comparing text.** `Rc` and `Arc` differ in nothing a program can observe except speed and `Send`-ness (`docs/rc-or-arc.md` §2), so a test that read the emitted Rust would pass on a lowering that does not run. `crates/nikaia/tests/shared.rs` lowers, compiles with the `rustc` that built the test, runs the binary and compares what it printed — at both settings of `user_parallelism`, which is the claim D6 rests on.
- **The guard that was waiting for this fired, and now asks the question it was for.** `a_std_entry_that_takes_a_shared_needs_a_second_look` refused any `std` signature mentioning `Shared`, because the analysis treats a described callee as accounted for. `Shared::deref` mentions it and is the shape the guard is not about: it takes `&Shared[$T]` and hands back `&$T`, so there is no handle for the entry to keep and nothing for it to cross a thread with. It now refuses a position that takes or hands back a **handle**, and a borrow of one is exempt.
- **One stale comment corrected.** `sysroot::lower_std_module` said `Shared` was what would break lowering `std` at one setting, citing [ADR-037](docs/specification/adr/adr-037.md) D3's per-build `Rc`-or-`Arc`. D6 took the representation off the switch, and the count a value gets never reads `user_parallelism`, so a `Shared` in `std`'s `.nika` half lowers to the same bytes at both settings.

### Fixed (a view keeps its type, and a transparent container is seen through)

- **`&` on a generic type answered nothing, so every view of one was unchecked** ([ADR-042](docs/specification/adr/adr-042.md) D1). `view_of` produced a type only where the type had no arguments, so `&Vec[i64]`, `&HashMap[&str, Stats]` and — the day it exists — `&Shared[Connection]` all came out as "nothing is known", the program was emitted, and a mismatch was reported by **rustc about the generated file**: `expected reference &str / found reference &Vec<i64>`, with a Rust `#[warn(unused_variables)]` note beside it. That is what Part III C.1 forbids, and it was not a gap in what the checker knew — the type was there and a step threw it away. A view now keeps its name and its arguments; a view of a view is the view; `&String` stays the one rewrite to `&str`; a tuple and a lambda still answer nothing, deliberately.
- **And a transparent container was refused, which is the opposite defect in the same place** ([ADR-042](docs/specification/adr/adr-042.md) D2). Part III says of `fs::Mapped` that *"a parser cannot tell the difference"* and `std.contracts` spells it — `fs::Mapped::deref` is `(&Mapped) -> &str` — but nothing consulted that entry, so a function taking a text view refused a mapped file, by this compiler's own `NK1102`. A type whose ledger records a `deref` is now seen through, **once**, and only to rescue a comparison that has already failed: it can turn a refusal into an acceptance and never the reverse, which is what makes it safe in a checker whose discipline is to say nothing where it knows nothing ([ADR-024](docs/specification/adr/adr-024.md)). The receiver's arguments bind the signature's variables as for any other method ([ADR-031](docs/specification/adr/adr-031.md)), so an entry written `(&Shared[$T]) -> &$T` will answer with what *this* container holds.
- **This is what [ADR-040](docs/specification/adr/adr-040.md) D1 was resting on, and D1 is corrected on first use with it.** It said a handle is duplicated where it is "handed on — passed to a function"; read literally that made every `serve(&db)` duplicate a handle, when a borrow hands nothing on and there is no atomic instruction to pay. Duplicated where the handle is passed **by value**; untouched where the inner value is lent out. Not a wording preference: it decides whether a function that only *uses* a shared value touches the count.
- **The first handle is made by writing the type** — `let db: Shared[Connection] = postgres::connect("…")`, no constructor and no method, because the language asks for sharing to be visible in the source and there it is, in the line where it starts. Only where the shared type stands in the same line may a plain value become a shared one; never at a call site merely because a signature wants one, since a call in which the word does not appear would move the cleanup point silently and there would be no saying whether the value was handed on or duplicated. `NK1115` says so and points at the line where the sharing belongs. Part I 6.2 also gains the borrowing rule and the `SharedMut` form — there is no `&` straight through a lock, so the caller opens it and passes the borrowed value in, and the called function must be `sync` and must touch no lock. All of that is specified ahead of the compiler: none of the three types exists, so `NK1115` is catalogued and never raised, while **the borrowing half is built** and exercised by `fs::Mapped`.

### Changed (named lambda arguments are the normal form)

- **`fn(user) { … }` is what a lambda looks like, and `fn { … }`'s automatic `a`, `b`, `c` are carried as experimental** ([ADR-041](docs/specification/adr/adr-041.md)). The automatic spelling hides a rule that is not visible in it: how many arguments the lambda takes is read off *which of the three names its body mentions*, so a local called `a` is not a local but an argument, and the closure's arity stops matching its call. `examples/access-log.nika` carried a comment warning its own reader off `c` for exactly that reason; the comment is gone because the trap is, not because it was documented. **`NK1114`** (warning, so the program still compiles) fires where one of the three is actually reached for, and names the mechanical rewrite — the same letters in a parameter list, after which the count is written down instead of read off the body. A `fn { … }` that reaches for none of them is **not** warned about: the form is not being withdrawn, the automatic naming is. The warning asks the same question the emitter asks to write the parameter list, from the same function (`emit::implicit_params`, now shared), so the two cannot disagree. Part I 5.2 and 5.3 swap the two options over, Part II 12.1/12.2/12.6, Part III 13.5/17.1 and six sites in four `examples/` programs are rewritten with names that say what the argument is, and [ADR-022](docs/specification/adr/adr-022.md) D1 is amended in which spelling is recommended and in nothing else — there is still one form with two spellings and both still parse everywhere. What this buys is the freedom to change the automatic rule at all, which is impossible while every lambda depends on it; whether it is eventually withdrawn is not decided.

### Added (four themes on the documentation site, and Nikaia code that is coloured like Nikaia)

- **The site offers System, Light, Dark and Sepia, and remembers which.** The control is in `_layouts/default.html` and the palettes are in `assets/css/nikaia.css` — one block of custom properties per theme, and below it the list of places a palette reaches, written once each. `jekyll-theme-primer` states its colours as literals, so a second theme could not be a variable swap inside the theme; it is a second stylesheet that names the theme's own places and reads their colour from a property. System is the *absence* of an attribute on `<html>` rather than a value of it, which is what makes a reader who has never touched the control, a reader with no JavaScript, and a reader who chooses System land in the same state. A stored choice is restored inline in the `<head>`, before the first paint, so a reader who chose Dark on a Light system does not see the white page first.

- **Sepia is there because of what this site is:** three specification parts and thirty-nine decision records, read end to end rather than searched. It is a light theme with the white taken out, and it reuses the light token colours rather than carrying a third set — which is only possible because the code block's background is taken off the block and given back from the palette.

- **The 180 ```` ```nika ```` blocks are highlighted from `editors/vscode/syntaxes/nikaia.tmLanguage.json`.** Jekyll highlights with Rouge, Rouge has no lexer for Nikaia, and the consequence was on every page of the specification: the Rust and TOML beside a Nikaia block were coloured and the Nikaia was not. What was missing was never a grammar — that one exists, and `editors/test/` has tested it against `vscode-textmate` all along — but somewhere to apply it outside an editor. `scripts/site-highlight/highlight.mjs` runs Shiki, which tokenizes with the same library, over `_site` after Jekyll and before the upload. What VS Code shows and what the site shows now come from one file and cannot drift apart.

- **Every block, not just the Nikaia ones.** 279 of them on 52 pages — 181 tokenized as Nikaia, the 180 ```` ```nika ```` fences and the one ```` ```nikaia ```` — and the `rust`, `toml`, `sh`, `bash`, `markdown` and `text` blocks too, so that one mechanism carries the reader's theme and no block is left on Rouge's fixed light colours. A fence naming a language Shiki does not know is set as plain text rather than failing. Both themes are written onto each token as custom properties with no literal `color`, because an inline colour outranks every selector in the stylesheet — that one decision is what keeps `nikaia.css` free of `!important` and lets the picker recolour code without a second copy of the page.

- **It asserts the shape it depends on instead of assuming it.** The input is generated HTML in two shapes — the wrapped form Rouge produces and the bare `<pre><code class="language-nika">` it produces for a language it cannot read — and the script fails the build if any `<pre>` reaches the end unconverted, rather than publishing a page where one block is themed and the next is not. That check earned itself immediately: it caught the block in `docs/technical_notes.md` that sits inside a list item, where Kramdown indents the wrappers and the two `</div>` are not adjacent.

- Verified by building the site the way `pages.yml` does and driving it in Chromium: all four themes, `prefers-color-scheme` in both states with no stored choice, the choice surviving a reload and a navigation, and the narrow-viewport layout where the control stops being fixed and becomes the first thing on the page.

### Fixed (the site was published without the theme it was configured to use)

- **Every page was a bare fragment: no `<head>`, no stylesheet, no viewport.** `_config.yml` has named `jekyll-theme-primer` since the site was set up, and `assets/css/style.css` was built and served on every deploy — 145 KB of it — with nothing on any page linking to it. The cause is that the site is deliberately made of Markdown files that carry no front matter, and a page that names no layout gets none; the theme was never reached. The `defaults` block added to `_config.yml` is what names it. The second entry in that block takes the layout back off `assets/`, where the theme's own `style.scss` carries front matter and would otherwise have been published wrapped in a page.

- **The logo was a 5 MB PNG named `.jpg`, on the front page.** `1768075880760.jpg` is 2816x1536 and is not a JPEG at all — browsers sniff the bytes, so it worked, and it cost every visitor five megabytes to see an image the README shows 300 pixels wide. `nikaia-logo.jpg` is the same picture at 600x327 and 24 KB, a real JPEG, with `height` on the tag so the page does not jump while it loads. The original stays in the repository and is no longer published: it is `exclude`d, which takes the built site from 8.8 MB to 3.7 MB.

- **The README now links to the site it publishes.** A `docs` badge and a `Documentation site` entry in the header navigation, both pointing at <https://keywan-ghadami.github.io/Nikaia/> — there was no way to reach the published documentation from the repository's front page.

- **The three README badges reached the site as literal text.** `![Version](…)` inside the centred `<div>` is Markdown inside a block-level HTML element, which GitHub renders and Kramdown does not. They are `<img>` now, which both render identically. It was there before and invisible, because there was no styled page to see it on.
### Fixed (`spawn` has one spelling, and the clone goes outside the task)

- **`spawn fn { … }` is the form in all three Parts.** Part III Appendix C.5's `NK2501` example wrote `spawn({ total(counts) })`, which made two normative documents disagree about the shape of the construct they were both describing. `spawn` is a call whose last argument is a lambda — the trailing lambda Part I 5.3 already has, and the same shape as the lock's blocking doors — so the parenthesised spelling is gone. It was the only site of that shape: a sweep of the three Parts, every note in `docs/`, `README.md` and `manifesto.md` for a lambda argument written inside a call's parentheses turns up one other line, Part II 12.8's `supervisor::start_link(fn { … }; restart_policy: "always")`, and that one is left exactly as it is — the lambda there is not the last argument, so there is no trailing form to move it to without deciding an argument order the specification has never written. It is the owner's. The entry in [`docs/spec-promises.md`](docs/spec-promises.md) says which half of its finding this closes and which half — the parser's `spawn "(" expr ")"` rule, untouched — still stands.

- **Three examples cloned inside the task, where the clone does nothing.** Part II 11.2 states that a `spawn` body's values are moved in when the task is built, and its own bullet says to call `.clone()` *before* spawning. Part I 8.3's example, the `help:` text of `NK2101` beside it and the second branch of Part II 12.7's `NK2102` help each wrote the clone inside the body instead — where it copies the task's own copy, leaves nothing for the parent, and in Part I's case carried a comment claiming the opposite. All three now bind the copy before the `spawn` and hand the task the copy. Nothing in `crates/` changed: `NK2101` and `NK2102` are catalogued and never raised, so the wrong help text existed only in the specification, and no test asserted it.

- **Part I 8.3 has a `Status` note**, which it needed and did not have: the move rule is written ahead of both halves it depends on — `spawn` does not lower and `NK2101` is not reported — and the parser still wants parentheses around the body.

### Added (why an automatic clone is not a convenience)

- [`docs/automatic-clone.md`](docs/automatic-clone.md) records the open question that the hand-written clone above invites, so that it is not mistaken for a comfort feature. A clone is one more owner and therefore a later cleanup, which is the observable change [ADR-037](docs/specification/adr/adr-037.md) D3 refuses to infer for `Shared` and what D7's rule sorts as written rather than inferred; [ADR-005](docs/specification/adr/adr-005.md) §3 rejects compiler-inserted clones outright and [ADR-008](docs/specification/adr/adr-008.md) D5 re-argues the ban for the view case. The note decides nothing and says what a proposal would have to answer.

### Changed (Parts I and II follow ADR-039: one shared mutable type, four doors, and nesting refused)

- **Six sentences were false or incomplete and are now the rule as [ADR-039](docs/specification/adr/adr-039.md) decides it.** Part II 12.3's "often a compile-time error" is **always** (D2); Part II 12.2's safety-net sentence claimed the re-entrant case is not refused statically and is replaced by the refusal plus the check's new role, self-control rather than error handling (D2, D8); Part I 1.2's "exactly two things to choose" is three (D8); Part I 6.2 defined `Locked[T]` by the nesting that no longer exists (D9); Part I 6.3's "you must use the `.access()` method" is four doors (D10); and Part II 12.2's derivation from the `sync` lambda is replaced by the stronger sentence — while the lock is open in `get` and `set`, no code of the user's runs at all (D10).

- **`SharedMut[T]` is the shared mutable type, from the start.** Part I 6.2 now says what each of `Shared[T]`, `SharedMut[T]` and `Locked[T]` is for — `Locked[T]` keeps its own use, individually locked fields inside a shared structure — and the hand-written `Shared[Locked[T]]` nesting is gone from every example in Parts I and II. Nothing is renamed: no program could write either type.

- **The four doors are in Part II 12.2, with the beginner's view in Part I 6.3**, each with the one sentence that says why it is shaped that way, plus the two refusals that come with them (assignment names `set`; a `set` whose argument reads the same container names `update`). Part II 12.2's ordering rule is refined rather than contradicted: `get` is a read and the other three are writes, so two `get`s are unordered ([ADR-033](docs/specification/adr/adr-033.md) D2) and the writing forms keep their order.

- **The lock-touching property is written down in Part II 12.3** — a second derived property per function beside `sync`, inferred over the same call graph, which is what catches chains and self-calls — together with why it is coarse, the warning never to answer 12.2's ordering question with it, and the rule that a function handed outward is judged by its body and never by its type. Part II 12.7 adds that opening a scope inside an open lock is refused, because the scope waits; Part II 11.2 adds that taking a lock inside a spawned task is the ordinary case, because it does not.

- **Eight `Status` notes say what is built, and nothing in this entry is.** Part I 1.2 (the third switch), 6.2 and 6.3; Part II 11.2, 12.2 (two of them), 12.3 and 12.7 — four of them new, four corrected. Neither type is known to the checker, no door exists, no function carries the property, and no refusal is reported. Part II 12.2's old note claimed the front end accepts `Locked[T]` and hands it to the backend; the probe in [ADR-039](docs/specification/adr/adr-039.md) §1 shows the name is an error where it is written, and the note now says that.

- **No rule was invented beyond the record.** [ADR-039](docs/specification/adr/adr-039.md) D6's foreign-call rule extends Part III 15.2's existing crossing sentence and is left to Part III. The diagnostics appendix needs codes for D2's nesting, D6's foreign call and D10's two refusals; the rules are written without a code until Appendix C allocates them.

- **`cargo test --workspace` 532 passed, 0 failed** (unchanged — no test reads these pages); `cargo fmt --all --check` clean; `scripts/check-adr-refs.py` exit 0. Specification only: no `.rs` file is touched and no behaviour changes.

### Assessed (what a full answer to a stored view would have to track, and what a half-built one does)

- **[`docs/stored-views.md`](docs/stored-views.md): an assessment, and it decides nothing.** What is built refuses a stored naked view or writes the subject's buffer out; the full rule is [ADR-008](docs/specification/adr/adr-008.md) D2's lattice, under which a stored view is neither an error nor a signature change but a **representation** change — the buffer takes a handle, the view becomes an offset and a length, and nothing is refused at all. The note asks what stands between the two, item by item.

- **Of the seven things it would need, two are present, one is computed and thrown away, and four are not.** Present: which fields of a type hold a view, and which parameters a result may point into — the latter from the *signature* only, so `borrows(a | b)` says nothing about which one. Computed and discarded: the checker infers a type for every `let` and `Checked` exports only findings, loops and method calls; the channel for handing one more answer to the emitter already exists. Absent: which parameter of a `std` call keeps what it is given, a buffer variable per view position, a solver over them, and any representation other than a native reference.

- **The decisive limit is that there is one buffer to name.** `crates/nikaia/src/emit/mod.rs` has a single `INPUT_LIFETIME`, and `contracts/trust.rs` already states the consequence in its own header. An analysis that proved a view came from a *different* buffer than its destination's would have nothing to emit, so it could only refuse — which is what the increment above already does for every destination it cannot name.

- **A half-built version was built and measured, which is why the note has a recommendation rather than a hunch.** The obvious next step — letting a local whose declared type holds a view be a destination — refuses `examples/k-nucleotide.nika`: 32 files lower instead of 33. That program writes views of its parameter into a map it hands back and is correct, because the container's buffer *is* the parameter's, and what says so is the `return`. No amount of following where the view goes answers that; equating two buffer variables does. So widening the reach before the solver exists converts working programs into refusals, and the order is forced. The probe was reverted and the corpus is back to 33/22.

- **Also recorded there, because it is a different defect and was left alone:** a free function taking a view-holding struct by value and handing one back fails with `E0106` whether or not the view is stored — the naked view is not what breaks it, so `NK2302` would be the wrong code to report. Nothing in the corpus writes the shape.

- No new ADR, no decision, and no change to any program: this entry is a note and a `docs/README.md` line.

### Changed (where the destination already names a buffer, the view is lowered instead of refused)

- **One step further along the same question, not a second mechanism.** `NK2302` asks where a naked view parameter's view goes. A **field** is declared, and its declaration says which buffer it points into ([ADR-008](docs/specification/adr/adr-008.md) D1) — so where the destination is a field of a subject that itself holds a view, the buffer is already named and the parameter is lowered as a view *of that buffer* rather than refused. `fn record(&mut self, name: &str, temp: i32)` on a `Summary` holding `HashMap[&str, Stats]` now emits `fn record(&mut self, name: &'a str, temp: i32)`, which compiles and runs — it is the program the `E0621` above was about. Part I 6.6's `Status` note and Appendix C.3's row say which cases are which.

- **The cost of the fail-closed direction is now a narrower signature rather than a refusal.** `self.names.contains_key(name)` was refused in the entry above, because nothing written down tells a call that keeps what it is given from one that does not. It lowers now, with `name` written as a view of the subject's buffer — the undecided case is still decided the safe way, and what the safe way costs is what a caller may pass instead of whether the program builds. `NK2302` therefore refuses no program the language below accepts.

- **Measured both ways on the corpus, which is the only reason this is here and not reasoned about.** Nothing that lowered before is refused now: 33 lower and 22 do not, the same files as before, and **the emitted Rust is byte-identical for all 33** — no program in the repository has the shape, so the change costs the corpus nothing in either direction. Nothing that was refused before lowers now either, for the same reason. The direction is shown on four programs in `crates/nikaia/tests/stored_views.rs` instead, each **compiled with the `rustc` that built the test and run**: the stored-into-a-field form, the stored-through-an-opaque-call form, the read-only call the entry above refused, and the one that reaches the field through a local. Reading the emitted text would not have caught this defect, because what the old behaviour produced was text that looked right.

- **Where the reach stops, as three tests rather than three sentences.** A function or method whose own subject holds no view still refuses, even when the destination is a field of a struct that carries one — there is no buffer in hand to write. A view handed back through the result still refuses: covering it means writing the result's buffer too, which is a second edit to the signature. A view given to a task still refuses. The caller side is unchecked: writing the subject's buffer on a parameter narrows what callers may pass, and nothing in the corpus hands such a method a shorter-lived view.

- **`cargo test --workspace` 532 passed, 0 failed** (529 before, 3 added and 2 converted from refusals to lowerings); `cargo fmt --all --check` clean; `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean; `scripts/check-adr-refs.py` exit 0. `nikaia.contracts` is unchanged: the ledger records Nikaia signatures, and `&str` is still what the source says. No new ADR.

### Fixed (a view kept past its call is refused in Nikaia, not in the generated Rust)

- **A function taking a naked `&str` and storing it lowered to Rust that `rustc` refused with `E0621`, naming the generated file** — which Part III C.1 calls a bug in this compiler rather than in the program. `fn record(&mut self, name: &str, temp: i32)` on a `Summary` holding `HashMap[&str, Stats]` emitted `fn record(&mut self, name: &str, …)` inside `impl<'a> Summary<'a>`, and the reader was told `help: add explicit lifetime 'a to the type of name` — every noun of which is something [ADR-008](docs/specification/adr/adr-008.md) D1 says the author never writes. The 0.0.9 entry below recorded this as D7's open half and said closing it needs ADR-008 D2's tether lattice; what it actually needed first was for Nikaia to have an answer of its own, and `NK2302` is that answer. The struct form — `fn record(&mut self, m: Reading)`, which `examples/1brc.nika` has used all along — was never broken, and is what the message shows.

- **`NK2302` (Part III, C.3; the rule is Part I 6.6).** `crates/nikaia/src/views.rs` asks, per naked view parameter, where its view goes, and reports four destinations: a field reached from `self` whose declared type holds a view, a field of a struct the function builds, the result where the result holds a view of a buffer that is not this parameter's, and a task's body. One refusal per parameter, at the place that says the most about where the view went.

- **It is wider than the rule, and that is the polarity rather than an oversight.** Where a carrier of the view is handed to a call on the subject, nothing written down says whether the callee keeps it — `insert` does, `contains_key` does not, and `std.contracts` records `key: ?` for both — so the call is reported as keeping it. Failing open there means emitting Rust that does not compile, which is the C.1 violation this exists to remove; [ADR-010](docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D1's "an analysis that fails open is a vulnerability generator" is the same argument with a different cost. The consequence is named in the code, in Part I 6.6's `Status` note and in C.3's row: `self.names.contains_key(name)` is refused today. `NK2302` is therefore the one code in Appendix C not covered by C.4's property, and C.3 says so.

- **The domain is narrow on purpose, which is not the same thing.** Only destinations whose buffer is known *not* to be the parameter's own count. `examples/k-nucleotide.nika`'s `fn count(seq: &str, k: usize) -> HashMap[&str, Tally]` writes views of `seq` into a map it hands back and is correct, because the result points into `seq` and nothing else; the result case asks exactly that question — is this parameter the only buffer the signature names — before it reports anything. A local whose declared type holds a view is **not** a destination, because deciding it needs to know which buffer each local views, which this compiler does not compute.

- **Measured, not assumed.** Every `.nika` in the repository still lowers: 33 lower and 22 do not, the same 22 as before (21 deliberately broken files in `tests/errors/`, plus `examples/fortunes.nika`, whose `dsl postgres` has no lowering). `no_program_in_the_repository_keeps_a_naked_view` walks `examples/`, `benches/` and the Nikaia half of `std` and finds nothing. `cargo test --workspace` 529 passed, 0 failed (520 before, 9 added); `cargo fmt --all --check` clean; `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean; `scripts/check-adr-refs.py` exit 0. No new ADR: the decision this rests on is ADR-008 D1, which already says a view in a field is a marker and a parameter gets no annotation.

- **Found on the way and *not* fixed, because it is a different defect.** A free function that takes a view-holding struct by value and hands one back — `fn record(s: Summary, name: &str) -> Summary` — emits `s: Summary<'_> … -> Summary<'_>` and fails with `E0106`, whether or not the view is stored: with a second naked view in the signature there is no single input for the result to be tied to. Nothing in the corpus writes that shape, and nothing here touches it: the naked view is not what breaks it, so `NK2302` would be the wrong code to report.

### Fixed (a trailing lambda can name its arguments)

- **The parser took `fn(user) { … }` inside a call's parentheses and only `fn { … }` outside them**, so Part I 5.3's own example, Part II 12.3's `account.access fn(to) { … }` and `access_all(a, b) fn(x, y) { … }` and Part II 12.7's `task::scope fn(s) { … }` were each read as two expressions - the call missing its last argument, and a closure passed to nobody, which in Rust type-checks often enough to be a different program rather than an error. `trailing_lambda` now shares `closure_params` with `closure_expr`, named arm first, and a trailing lambda reaches a path as well as a method call. [ADR-022](docs/specification/adr/adr-022.md) D1's "one lambda form" is unchanged and not reopened: naming the arguments is that form spelled out. The Status notes in Part I 5.3, Part II 12.3 and Part II 12.7 say so, `crates/nikaia/tests/lambdas.rs` compiles the emitted Rust for each shape, and the three re-run rows in [`docs/spec-promises.md`](docs/spec-promises.md) correct what the 0.0.9 entry below reported about them. Still not built, and now tests of their own: an effect marker on a lambda (`fn(info) sync { … }`, Part I 7.2) has no grammar at all, and `spawn fn(x) { … }` is one call to a function named `spawn` where it used to be two statements naming a value - the same `E0425`, one noun closer to the truth.

### Measured (0.0.9 — what an always-`Mutex` floor for `Locked` would cost against Part II 12.2)

- **An investigation, and it decides nothing about `Locked[T]`'s representation.** Part II 12.2 gives the lock two implementations, one per `user_parallelism`, and forbids the program pausing while something is locked — so a `Mutex` floor at *both* settings has to be cleared against that rule first.
